Homomorphism Indistinguishability Relations induced by Quantum Groups
Tim Seppelt, Gian Luca Spitzer

Abstract
Homomorphism indistinguishability is a way of characterising many natural equivalence relations on graphs. Two graphs G G and H H are called homomorphism indistinguishable over a graph class ℱ \mathcal{F} if for each F ∈ ℱ F\in\mathcal{F} , the number of homomorphisms from F F to G G equals the number of homomorphisms from F F to H H . Examples of such equivalence relations include isomorphism and cospectrality, as well as equivalence with respect to many formal logics. Quantum groups are a generalisation of topological groups that describe “non-commutative symmetries” and, inter alia, have applications in quantum information theory. An important subclass are the easy quantum groups, which enjoy a combinatorial characterisation and have been fully classified by Raum and Weber. A recent connection between these seemingly distant concepts was made by Mančinska and Roberson, who showed that quantum isomorphism, a relaxation of classical isomorphism that can be phrased in terms of the quantum symmetric group, is equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over the class of planar graphs. We generalise Mančinska and Roberson’s result to all orthogonal easy quantum groups. We obtain for each orthogonal easy quantum group a graph isomorphism relaxation ≈ \approx and a graph class ℱ \mathcal{F} , such that homomorphism indistinguishability over ℱ \mathcal{F} coincides with ≈ \approx . Our results include a full classification of the ( 0 , 0 ) (0,0) -intertwiners of the graph-theoretic quantum group obtained by adding the adjacency matrix of a graph to the intertwiners of an orthogonal easy quantum group.



\declaretheorem

[name=Theorem,numberwithin=chapter,sibling=theorem]thm

1
Introduction

Homomorphism counts capture graph structure. More precisely, two graphs
G
and
H
are said to be
homomorphism indistinguishable
over a class of graphs
\mathcal{F}
if for all graphs
F\in\mathcal{F}
, the number of homomorphisms from
F
to
G
equals the number of homomorphisms from
F
to
H
. In 1967, Lovász
[
11
]
first showed that isomorphism is equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over all graphs. Since then, a wide spectrum of equivalence relations have been characterised as homomorphism indistinguishability relations. Examples include natural equivalence relations such as cospectrality and having the same degree sequence, as well as
equivalences in different formal logics
[
6
,
8
,
7
]
, results in category theory
[
4
,
15
]
, and characterisations of different linear programming relaxations
[
5
,
9
,
19
]
, cf. the monograph
[
21
]
.

Particularly interesting is a recent result by Mančinska and Roberson
[
14
]
, who showed that
quantum isomorphism
is equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over the class of planar graphs. Quantum isomorphism was originally defined in
[
1
]
in terms of the
graph isomorphism game
. This is a non-local game in which two players have to convince a verifier that two graphs are isomorphic. They are not allowed to communicate, but may agree on a strategy beforehand. Classically, the players have a winning strategy if and only if the graphs are in fact isomorphic. On the other hand, we call the two graphs
quantum isomorphic
if the players have a winning strategy when being allowed to coordinate their answers by measuring a shared entangled quantum state.

Results by Lupini, Mančinska, and Roberson
[
12
]
show that classical and quantum and isomorphism share deep similarities. Classical isomorphism of graphs
G
and
H
can be equivalently phrased as the existence of a permutation matrix
P
such that
PA_{G}=A_{H}P
, where
A_{G},A_{H}
are the adjacency matrices of
G
and
H
respectively. Analogously, quantum isomorphism turns out to be equivalent to the existence of a so-called
quantum permutation matrix

\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
. Classically, if
G
and
H
are connected, then they are isomorphic if and only if there exists an orbit of the automorphism group of the disjoint union
G\cup H
, that intersects both
G
and
H
non-trivially. As such, classical isomorphism is closely related to the symmetric group
S_{n}
and automorphism groups. Again, an analogous picture turns out to hold for quantum isomorphism: Two connected graphs are quantum isomorphic if and only if there exists an orbit of the
quantum automorphism group
of
G\cup H
that intersects
G
and
H
non-trivially. Quantum isomorphism is thus closely related to the
quantum symmetric group

S^{+}_{n}
and quantum automorphism groups
\Qut(\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}})
.

Quantum groups such as the quantum symmetric group generalise classical groups and can be seen as describing “non-commutative symmetries”. They have their origins in mathematical physics and have been extensively studied. Mančinska and Roberson showed that the intertwiners of a certain subclass of quantum groups, among which the
orthogonal easy quantum groups
, can be seen as
homomorphism tensors
, which are matrices that keep track of homomorphism counts from bilabelled graphs. Through a Tannaka-Krein-type duality originally due to Woronowicz
[
29
]
, the bilabelled graphs in question then entirely determine the quantum group. For the quantum symmetric group, these graphs are shown to be planar bilabelled graphs, which eventually yields the equivalence of quantum isomorphism and homomorphism indistinguishability over planar graphs.

In light of these connections, a natural question is whether similar homomorphism indistinguishability results can be obtained for other orthogonal easy quantum groups, which have, in fact, been fully classified by Raum and Weber
[
17
]
. We answer this question affirmatively. We introduce
graph instantiations
of easy quantum groups, which generalise automorphism and quantum automorphism groups of graphs, and eventually prove the following theorem.

Theorem
1

(Informal)
.

Let
Q
be an orthogonal easy quantum group and let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of its intertwiner graphs. Then for all graphs
G
and
H
, the following are equivalent.

1.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the unlabelled graphs in
\mathcal{F}
.

2.

There exists a matrix
\mathcal{U}
with entries in some C
∗
-algebra, such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M=M\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for all intertwiners
M
of
Q
and suitable
k,\ell
.

We will see that the constraints imposed on
\mathcal{U}
by the intertwiners of
Q
correspond to very natural properties of matrices, such as orthogonality or restrictions on row and column sums.
For example, if we take
Q
to be the quantum symmetric group,
then
\mathcal{F}
is the class of all planar graphs.
In this case, we recover the result of Mančinska and Roberson
[
14
]
that
G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over all planar graphs if and only if there is a quantum permutation matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.
Notably, this argument does not rely on quantum orbits.

Based on Raum and Weber’s classification, we finally identify the classes of unlabelled intertwiner graphs of all easy quantum groups.
We find that for every orthogonal easy quantum group, these graph classes are either all graphs, all planar graphs, all paths and cycles, or all cycles (
Theorem

5.115
).
This yields several concrete corollaries of our theorem and partially answers a question by Mančinska and Roberson.

2
Background

2.1
Graphs and Homomorphisms

A
graph

G
is a set
V(G)
of vertices equipped with a binary relation
E(G)\subseteq V(G)^{2}
defining the edges. Unless otherwise specified, we assume all graphs to be
simple
: they are undirected and do not contain loops, that is,
E(G)
is irreflexive and symmetric. We usually write
uv
or
vu
to denote the edge
(u,v)=(v,u)\in E(G)
.
We can define a matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{V(G)\times V(G)}
with
A_{uv}=1\Leftrightarrow uv\in E(G)
, which is called the
adjacency matrix
of
G
. The class of all graphs is denoted by
\mathcal{G}
.

We call
H
a
subgraph
of
G
if
H
can be obtained from
G
by removing vertices and edges. A graph is
connected
if there exists a path between any two vertices. The
disjoint union

G\cup H
of
G
and
H
is the graph given by
(V(G)\sqcup V(H),E(G)\sqcup E(H))
, where
\sqcup
denotes the disjoint union of sets. We
subdivide
an edge
uv\in E(G)
by removing it, adding a new vertex
w
and adding the edges
uw
and
wv
. If
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
is a path in
G
such that
v_{i}
has degree
2
for all
1<i<k
, we
unsubdivide
it by removing the inner vertices and adding the edge
v_{1}v_{k}
. We
contract
an edge
uv\in E(G)
by removing it, identifying
u
and
v
, and suppressing any resulting multiple edges.

A
planar embedding
of a graph
G
consists of an injective function
f\colon V(G)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a set of curves
h_{uv}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
for all
uv\in E(G)
such that
h_{uv}(0)=f(u)
and
h_{uv}(1)=f(v)
, and for distinct edges
e,e^{\prime}\in E(G)
we have
h_{e}(x)\neq h_{e^{\prime}}(y)
except for
x,y\in\{0,1\}
. A graph is called
planar
if it has a planar embedding, or informally if it can be drawn on the plane without any edges crossing. The
faces
of planar embedding are the connected regions of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\bigcup_{e\in E(G)}\img h_{e}
. A
facial cycle
is a cycle in
G
that is the boundary of a face in some planar embedding.

Convention
.

For simplicity, we will assume that
V(G)=[\lvert V(G)\rvert]
. This means that the class of all graphs is a set, and that the adjacency matrix of a graph uniquely corresponds to a matrix
\mathbb{C}^{\lvert V(G)\rvert\times\lvert V(G)\rvert}
. Note that this is done without loss of generality, since every graph is isomorphic to a graph of this form and all our results will be isomorphism-invariant.

A
graph homomorphism
from a graph
G
to a graph
H
is a mapping
h\colon V(G)\to V(H)
that preserves adjacency, that is,
uv\in E(G)
implies
h(u)h(v)\in E(H)
. For any graph
F
, we denote by
\HOM(F,G)
the set of homomorphisms from
F
to
G
, and let
\hom(F,G)\coloneqq\lvert\HOM(F,G)\rvert
. We then say that
G
and
H
are
homomorphism indistinguishable
over a class of graphs
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
if
\hom(F,G)=\hom(F,H)
for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
.
See
[
21
]
for background on homomorphism indistinguishability.

2.2
Easy Quantum Groups

Our main objects of study will be easy quantum groups. We give a short overview here, for a more in-depth treatment see for example the monograph
[
13
]
. Easy quantum groups are a subclass of compact quantum groups, which in turn generalise compact classical groups, that is, topological groups whose underlying space
X
is compact and Hausdorff. Through Gelfand duality, we may consider instead of
X
the commutative C
∗
-algebra
C(X)
of complex-valued continuous functions on
X
. Now a group multiplication
m\colon X\times X\to X
induces a
comultiplication

\Delta\colon C(X)\to C(X)\otimes C(X)\cong C(X\times X)
as

\Delta(f)(x,y)=f(xy),

that is coassociative, that is,
(\id\circ\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\circ\id)\circ\Delta
, and that satisfies that the linear spans of
\Delta(C(X))(\mathbf{1}\otimes C(X))
and
\Delta(C(X))(C(X)\otimes\mathbf{1})
are dense in
C(X)\otimes C(X)
.
Importantly, one can show that, from every
*
-homomomorphism
C(X)\to C(X)\otimes C(X)
satisfying these properties, one can uniquely recover a group multiplication
m\colon X\times X\to X
. Consequently, one may view compact groups equivalently as commutative C
∗
-algebras equipped with such a
*
-homomorphism. We define compact quantum groups by dropping the commutativity requirement.

Definition 1
.

A
compact quantum group

G
is given by a tuple
(C(G),\Delta)
consisting of a C
∗
-algebra
C(G)
and a
∗
-homomorphism
\Delta\colon C(G)\to C(G)\otimes C(G)
, called comultiplication, such that

1.

(\id\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta
.

2.

\Delta(C(G))(\mathbf{1}\otimes C(G))
and
\Delta(C(G))(C(G)\otimes\mathbf{1})
are linearly dense in
C(G)\otimes C(G)
.

Remark 2
.

The name “quantum group” may be justified by noting that quantum mechanics can be derived from classical mechanics in essentially the same way, see for example
[
23
]
. In particular, non-commutativity is
the
defining feature of quantum mechanics.

Along similar lines, we may generalise compact matrix groups, that is, compact groups
G
admitting a faithful matrix representation
\chi\colon G\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Their corresponding function algebras turn out to be generated by the coordinate functions

u_{ij}\colon G\to\mathbb{C},x\mapsto\chi(x)_{ij},

for which the comultiplication will be of the form

\Delta(u_{ij})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{ik}\otimes u_{kj}.

A few additional properties of the
u_{ij}
allow recovering the original matrix representation. Compact matrix quantum groups are then defined by dropping the commutativity requirement.

Definition 3
.

A
compact matrix quantum group

G
is given by a tuple
(C(G),\mathcal{U},n)
, consisting of a C
∗
-algebra
C(G)
, an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a matrix
\mathcal{U}=(u_{ij})\in C(G)^{n\times n}
, called the
fundamental representation
of
G
, such that

1.

The elements
\{u_{ij}\mid i,j\in[n]\}
generate
C(G)
,

2.

The matrix
\mathcal{U}
is unitary and
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}}
is invertible,

3.

The map
\Delta\colon C(G)\to C(G)\otimes C(G),u_{ij}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{ik}\otimes u_{kj}
is a
∗
-homomorphism, called comultiplication.

Remark 4
.

The definition of the
u_{ij}
suggests to arrange them as a matrix, that is, to view them as an element
\mathcal{U}\in C(G)^{n\times n}
. We can then define the standard matrix terminology as one would expect. The transpose of
\mathcal{U}=(u_{ij})
is
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}}=(u_{ji})
, the adjoint
\mathcal{U}^{*}
is the conjugate transpose, and the identity matrix in
C(G)^{n\times n}
is
\mathbf{1}I
.
We may equivalently see
\mathcal{U}
as an element of
C(G)\otimes B(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.

Example 2.5
.

Wang
[
27
]
defines the
quantum orthogonal group
as the compact matrix quantum group
O_{n}^{+}
, whose underlying C
∗
-algebra is the universal C
∗
-algebra generated by the
n^{2}
entries of
\mathcal{U}=(u_{ij})
, subject to the conditions

u_{ij}^{*}=u_{ij}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathcal{U}=\mathbf{1}\mathcal{U}.

(1)

Example 2.6
.

Another compact matrix quantum group introduced by Wang
[
28
]
is the
quantum symmetric group

S_{n}^{+}
, whose underlying C
∗
-algebra is the universal C
∗
-algebra generated by the
n^{2}
entries of
\mathcal{U}=(u_{ij})
, such that

u_{ij}^{2}=u_{ij}^{*}=u_{ij}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{k}u_{ik}=\mathbf{1}=\sum_{k}u_{kj}.

(2)

Note that these are exactly the conditions that define permutation matrices in the case where
u_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
.

In this paper, we will mostly be interested in quantum subgroups of the quantum orthogonal group, that is, compact matrix quantum groups whose fundamental representation satisfies
Equation

1
. These
orthogonal
compact matrix quantum groups turn out to have a particularly accessable representation theory.

Definition 2.7
.

A
finite-dimensional representation
of a compact matrix quantum group
G
is a matrix
\mathcal{V}=(v_{ij})\in C(G)^{m\times m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying

\Delta(v_{ij})=\sum_{k}v_{ik}\otimes v_{kj}.

Definition 2.8
.

An
intertwiner
is an equivariant map between two finite-dim-ensional representations
\mathcal{U}\in C(G)^{m\times m}
,
\mathcal{V}\in C(G)^{m^{\prime}\times m^{\prime}}
, that is, a linear map
T\colon\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}^{m^{\prime}}
such that
T\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{V}T
.

We can arrange the finite-dimensional representations of a compact matrix quantum group
G
into a category, called the
representation category

\Rep(G)
.

Objects:

finite-dimensional unitary representations of
G
,

Morphisms:

intertwiners,

Composition:

natural composition.

The following result shows that
\Rep(G)
is completely determined by the fundamental representation of
G
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
G=(C(G),\mathcal{U},n)
be an orthogonal compact matrix quantum group. Then

1.

Every invertible finite-dimensional representation
G
is equivalent to a unitary representation of
G
.

2.

Every unitary representation of
G
decomposes into a direct sum of irreducible representations of
G
.

3.

Every unitary irreducible representation of
G
is either the trivial representation
(\mathbf{1})
or equivalent to a direct summand of a tensor power of
\mathcal{U}
.

This suggests to define the
reduced representation category

\Rep_{0}(G)
as the full subcategory of
\Rep(G)
consisting of the tensor powers of
\mathcal{U}
. One can show that both
\Rep(G)
and
\Rep_{0}(G)
are rigid monoidal categories where the monoid product is the tensor product, the monoidal unit is the trivial representation
(\mathbf{1})
, and the dual of a representation
\mathcal{V}=(v_{ij})
is given by
\overline{\mathcal{V}}=(v_{ij}^{*})
. One may further show that the morphisms of
\Rep_{0}(G)
form a so-called
concrete tensor category with duals
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A
concrete tensor category with duals

C
is a collection of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces
C(\ell,k)\subseteq\{T\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes\ell}~\text{linear}\}
for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

1.

if
T\in C(\ell,k)
and
T^{\prime}\in C(r,s)
, then
T\otimes T^{\prime}\in C(\ell+r,k+s)
,

2.

if
T\in C(\ell,k)
and
T^{\prime}\in C(k,r)
, then
TT^{\prime}\in C(\ell,r)
,

3.

if
T\in C(\ell,k)
, then
T^{*}\in C(k,\ell)
,

4.

I\in C(1,1)
,

5.

\xi\coloneqq\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\in C(2,0)
.

This allows us to state a central result in the theory of compact matrix quantum groups: a Tannaka-Krein-type duality due to Woronowicz
[
29
]
, which allows to recover a compact matrix quantum group from its finite-dimensional representations.

Theorem 2.11

(Woronowicz-Tannaka-Krein)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
C
be a concrete tensor category with duals. Then there exists a unique compact matrix quantum group
(C(G),\mathcal{U},n)
with

\operatorname{Mor}_{\Rep_{0}(G)}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell})=C(\ell,k)

for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

In particular, we can reason about compact matrix quantum groups entirely through their intertwiners. We therefore introduce the following simplified notation.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
(C(G),\mathcal{U},n)
be a compact matrix quantum group. We denote by
C^{G}
the concrete tensor category with duals formed by the morphisms of
\Rep_{0}(G)
and refer to it as the
intertwiners of
G
. Similarly, we refer to the sets
C^{G}(\ell,k)
as the
(\ell,k)
-intertwiners of
G
.

Finally, we can introduce
easy quantum groups
. These are compact matrix quantum groups whose intertwiners can be described combinatorically. Again, we are interested in the orthogonal case.

Definition 2.13
.

A
(\ell,k)
-partition
is a partition of the set
\{1_{L},\dots,\ell_{L},1_{U},\dots,k_{U}\}
, where we think of
1_{L},\dots,\ell_{L}
as the
lower points
, and of
1_{U},\dots,k_{U}
as the
upper points
of the partition. We denote the set of these partitions by
\mathbb{P}(\ell,k)
.

We can define such a partition graphically by drawing the upper points above the lower points and connecting points that are in the same block of the partition, as shown in
Figure

1
.

P_{1}=\{\{1_{L}\},\{1_{U},2_{U},2_{L}\}
,
\{3_{U},4_{L}\},\{4_{U},3_{L}\}\}
P_{2}=\{\{1_{U},1_{L},2_{L},3_{L}\}
,
\{2_{U},3_{U},4_{U}\}\}

Figure 1:
Drawing partitions

An important subclass are
non-crossing
partitions, which are the partitions that can be drawn without any lines crossing.

Definition 2.14
.

A partition
P
of an ordered set
V
is called
non-crossing
if whenever
a<b<c<d
, and
a,c
are in the same part and
b,d
are in the same part, then the two parts coincide. We define non-crossing partitions of
\{1_{L},\dots,\ell_{L},1_{U},\dots,k_{U}\}
by letting
1_{L}<\dots<\ell_{L}<1_{U}<\dots<k_{U}
.

It is possible to define certain operations on partitions. Let
P\in\mathbb{P}(\ell,k)
and
P^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime})
. Then the tensor product
P\otimes P^{\prime}\in P(\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime})
is the partition obtained by drawing
P
and
P^{\prime}
next to each other horizontally, as shown in
Figure

2
. If
\ell^{\prime}=k
, then the composition
P^{\prime}\circ P\in\mathbb{P}(k^{\prime},\ell)
is obtained by drawing
P^{\prime}
below
P
and identifying the lower row of
P
and the upper row of
P^{\prime}
. Note that this operation may create
loops
, i.e. blocks that are no longer connected to the upper or lower row of the resulting partition. These become
empty blocks
of
P^{\prime}\circ P
. Finally, the
adjoint

P^{*}\in\mathbb{P}(k,\ell)
is obtained by reflecting
P
across the horizontal axis.

\otimes
=
\circ
=
=
\bBigg@{3}(
\bBigg@{3})^{*}
=

Figure 2:
Operations on partitions

To any partition
P\in\mathbb{P}(\ell,k)
and
n\geq 1
, one can associate a linear map
T_{P}^{(n)}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes\ell}
as follows. For
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{\ell},\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
, we let
\delta(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=1
if the assignments
i_{U}\mapsto b_{i}
and
i_{L}\mapsto a_{i}
are constant on the blocks of
P
, and
\delta(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=0
otherwise.
Then
T_{P}^{(n)}
is defined on basis vectors
e_{b_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{b_{k}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}
as

T_{P}^{(n)}(e_{b_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{b_{k}})=n^{e(P)}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{\ell}}\delta(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})(e_{a_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{a_{\ell}}),

where
e(P)
denotes the number of empty blocks in
P
. This definition has a nice graphical interpretation, where we simply shuffle the input vectors according to the blocks, see
Figure

3
. This suggest to write the matrix form of
T_{P}^{(n)}
in terms of outer products: Consider, for instance, the partition
P_{1}
in
Figure

1
. The corresponding matrix can be seen to be equal to

T_{P_{1}}^{(n)}=\sum_{ijk\ell}(e_{\ell}\otimes e_{i}\otimes e_{k}\otimes e_{j})\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}.

e_{1}
\otimes
e_{2}
\otimes
e_{3}
\otimes
e_{3}
e_{2}
\otimes
e_{3}
\otimes
e_{1}
e_{1}
\otimes
e_{1}
\otimes
e_{2}
\otimes
e_{3}
(\sum_{k}e_{k})
\otimes
e_{1}
\otimes
e_{3}
\otimes
e_{2}

Partitions as linear maps

Example 2.15
.

The linear maps corresponding to the single block partitions
\{\{1_{L},\dots,\ell_{L},1_{U},\dots k_{U}\}\}\in\mathbb{P}(\ell,k)
map
k
copies of a vector to
\ell
copies. We call these maps the
generalised multiplication maps
and denote them by
M^{(n),\ell,k}
. Two important special cases are
M^{(n),1,1}=I_{n}
and
M^{(n),2,0}=\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}=\xi
.

Example 2.16
.

Another important partition is the
swap
partition
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\{2_{U},1_{L}\}\}
. Its corresponding linear map is
S^{(n)}\colon e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\mapsto e_{j}\otimes e_{i}
.

Example 2.17
.

We represent the single point partitions
\{\{1_{L}\}\}
and
\{\{1_{U}\}\}
graphically as
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
, to distinguish them from each other and from the identity partition

.

Banica and Speicher
[
3
]
showed that the operations of tensor product, composition, and adjoint on partitions coincide with the corresponding operations on linear maps.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}
. Then we have
T_{P\otimes P^{\prime}}=T_{P}\otimes T_{P^{\prime}}
,
T_{P^{*}}=T_{P}^{*}
, and if
P
and
P^{\prime}
are compatible,
T_{P\circ P^{\prime}}=T_{P}\circ T_{P^{\prime}}
.

This allows us to define
partition categories
that give rise to concrete tensor categories with duals.

Definition 2.19
.

A
partition category

\mathcal{C}
is a set of partitions, such that

1.

if
P\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,k)
and
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(r,s)
, then
P\otimes P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\ell+r,k+s)
,

2.

if
P\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,k)
and
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(k,r)
, then
P\circ P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,r)
,

3.

if
P\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,k)
, then
P^{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,r)
,

4.

\{\{1_{U},1_{L}\}\}=\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

,

5.

\{\{1_{L},2_{L}\}\}=\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

under tensor products, composition, and taking adjoints.
Now it is easy to see that for every partition category
\mathcal{C}
, the collection of vector spaces

C(\ell,k)=\lspan\{T_{P}\mid P\in\mathcal{C}(\ell,k)\}

forms a concrete tensor category with duals.
Theorem

2.11
thus implies the following result.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a partition category. Then for each
n\geq 1
there exists a unique compact matrix quantum group
G_{n}(\mathcal{C})=(A,\mathcal{U},n)
such that

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T_{P}=T_{P}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}

for all
P\in\mathcal{C}(k,\ell)
.

Compact matrix quantum groups of this form are called
orthogonal easy quantum groups
. Indeed, it follows from
[
13
, Remark 3.2.15]
that
\mathcal{U}
is always orthogonal. Moreover, one can prove that
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}S=S\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}
if and only if the entries of
\mathcal{U}
commute. Hence, an easy quantum group
G_{n}(\mathcal{C})
is classical if and only if
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\in\mathcal{C}
.

2.3
Classification of Orthogonal Easy Quantum Groups

The orthogonal easy quantum groups have been fully classified by Raum and Weber
[
17
]
. Since we will work extensively with this classification, we restate it here for the reader’s convenience.

The cases in (i), (ii), (iii) are pairwise distinct.

As noted above, these partition categories form tensor categories with duals and thus uniquely correspond to compact matrix quantum groups. Moreover, the compact matrix quantum groups with intertwiner

have commutative C
∗
-algebras and thus correspond to classical groups.

Proposition 2.22
.

The easy groups, i.e. the easy quantum groups that correspond to partition categories
\mathcal{C}
with
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 39.37 62.99 M 39.37 0 L 0 62.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\in\mathcal{C}
are

1.

G_{n}(P)=S_{n}
, the symmetric group,

2.

G_{n}(P_{2})=O_{n}
, the orthogonal group,

3.

G_{n}(P_{even})=H_{n}
, the hyperoctahedral group, i.e. the group of monomial orthogonal matrices,

4.

G_{n}(P^{\prime})=S^{\prime}_{n}
, the modified symmetric group, i.e. the group of signed permutation matrices,

5.

G_{n}(P_{b})=B_{n}
, the bistochastic group, i.e. the group of orthogonal matrices having row and column sum
1
,

6.

G_{n}(P_{b}^{\prime})=B_{n}^{\prime}
, the modified bistochastic group, i.e. the group of signed bistochastic matrices.

Here a matrix is called
monomial
if it has the same zero pattern as a permutation matrix, and a signed permutation (bistochastic) matrix is a permutation (bistochastic) matrix multiplied by
\pm 1
.
Note that the term “bistochastic” is sometimes used to refer to a matrix all whose row and column sums are
1
. In this article,
bistochastic
means additionally that the matrix is orthogonal.

We obtain “liberated” quantum versions of these groups by dropping the commutativity constraint. This corresponds to dropping the

partition from the generating sets.

Proposition 2.23
.

The easy quantum groups that correspond to categories of non-crossing partitions are

1.

G_{n}(NC)=S_{n}^{+}
, the quantum symmetric group,

2.

G_{n}(NC_{2})=O_{n}^{+}
, the quantum orthogonal group,

3.

G_{n}(NC_{even})=H_{n}^{+}
, the quantum hyperoctahedral group,

4.

G_{n}(NC^{\prime})=S_{n}^{\prime+}
, the quantum modified symmetric group,

5.

G_{n}(NC_{b})=B_{n}^{+}
, the quantum bistochastic group,

6.

G_{n}(NC_{b}^{\prime})=B_{n}^{\prime+}
, the quantum complexly modified bistochastic group,

7.

G_{n}(NC_{b}^{\#})=B_{n}^{\#+}
, the quantum modified bistochastic group.
