. Dropping the commutativity constraint, however, yields the distinct easy quantum groups
B_{n}^{\prime+}
and
B^{\#+}_{n}
respectively.

Remark 2.24
.

In the literature on easy quantum groups,
B_{n}^{\prime+}
and
B_{n}^{\#+}
are usually called “quantum modified bistochastic group” and “quantum freely modified bistochastic group” respectively. However, as we will see later, the intertwiner
\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
captures the notion of signed bistochastic matrices in a much more intuitive way than

. It thus makes sense to instead call
B_{n}^{\#+}
the quantum modified bistochastic group. The name “quantum complexly modified bistochastic group” is justified by the fact that
B_{n}^{\prime+}
is the so-called
tensor 2-complexification
of
B_{n}^{+}
, see
[
24
, Remark 4.14]
.

2.4
Graph Homomorphisms and the Quantum Symmetric Group

Mančinska and Roberson
[
14
]
discovered a surprising connection between these quantum groups and graph homomorphisms. Concretely, the intertwiners of easy quantum groups are equivalently characterised by
homomorphism tensors
which count homomorphisms from bilabelled graphs.

As the name suggests, bilabelled graphs are graphs whose vertices may be annotated by two types of labels, the
in-labels
and the
out-labels
. A vertex can have both in-labels and out-labels and not every vertex has to be labelled.

Definition 2.25
.

A
(k,\ell)
-
bilabelled graph

\mathbf{K}
is a tuple
(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
where
K
is a graph,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in V(K)^{k}
is the
output vector
, and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})\in V(K)^{\ell}
is the
input vector
. We let
V(\mathbf{K})=V(K)
and
E(\mathbf{K})=E(K)
. We denote the set of all
(k,\ell)
-bilabelled graphs by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
.

One may think of the labels as elements in
[k]
and
[\ell]
respectively, where a vertex
v\in V(K)
has out-label
i\in[k]
if
a_{i}=v
, and in-label
j\in[\ell]
if
b_{j}=v
. In this sense, we will also use the following definition.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
be a bilabelled graph. We denote by
l^{\mathbf{K}}_{in}(v)\subseteq[\ell]
,
l^{\mathbf{K}}_{out}(v)\subseteq[k]
, and
l^{\mathbf{K}}(v)\subseteq[\ell]\sqcup[k]
the sets of in-labels, out-labels, and all labels of
v
respectively. We omit the superscript when the graph is clear from the context.

When drawing a bilabelled graph, the labels are represented as grey “wires” that are ordered from top to bottom, where input wires extend to the right and output wires to the left, as seen in
Figure

4
.

1
2
3
4
5
\mathbf{K}=(K_{1,4},(3,4,1),(1,5,5))
1
2
3
4
\mathbf{K^{\prime}}=(K_{4},(3,4,1),(3,4))

Figure 4:
Drawing bilabelled graphs

Based on the labels, it is possible to define tensor products, composition, and adjoints of bilabelled graphs. Let
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell,k)
and
\mathbf{K^{\prime}}=(K^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell^{\prime},k^{\prime})
. Then the tensor product
\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{K^{\prime}}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell+\ell^{\prime},k+k^{\prime})
is defined as the bilabelled graph
(K\cup K^{\prime},\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
. If
\ell^{\prime}=k
, the composition
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k^{\prime},\ell)
is formed by constructing the disjoint union
K\cup K^{\prime}
, identifying the vertices
a_{i}^{\prime}
and
b_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
, and removing any duplicate edges but keeping the loops. Finally, the adjoint
\mathbf{K}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
is given by
(K,\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a})
.
We denote the closure of a set of bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{K}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{K}_{n}
under these operations by
\langle{}\mathbf{K}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{K}_{n}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
.
As for partitions, we can view the operations graphically, as shown in
Figure

5
. Composition, in particular, corresponds to drawing
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
on the left of
\mathbf{K}
so that the output and input wires align, connecting the wires, and then contracting the resulting edges.

\circ
=
\otimes
=
\bBigg@{4}(
\bBigg@{4})^{*}
=

Figure 5:
Operations on bilabelled graphs

Homomorphisms between bilabelled graphs are required to preserve labels.

Definition 2.27
.

A
bilabelled graph homomorphism
from
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
to
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(K^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
is a homomorphism
h\colon K\to K^{\prime}
such that
h(\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
and
h(\mathbf{b})=\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
.

We count homomorphisms from a bilabelled graph to an unlabelled graph by considering all possible label positions in the target graph, and arranging the resulting numbers of bilabelled graph homomorphisms in a tensor.

Definition 2.28
.

Given a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell,k)
and a graph
G
, the
G
-homomorphism tensor
of
\mathbf{K}
is the
V(G)^{\ell}\times V(G)^{k}
matrix
\mathbf{K}_{G}
with entries

(\mathbf{K}_{G})_{\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}}=\lvert\{h\colon\mathbf{K}\to(G,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\mid h\text{ bilabelled graph homomorphism}\}\rvert.

Example 2.29
.

Let
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell,k)
be a bilabelled graph, and
G
be an unlabelled graph. Then we have
\hom(K,G)=\soe(\mathbf{K}_{G})
, where

\soe(\mathbf{K}_{G})=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in V(G)^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in V(G)^{k}}(\mathbf{K}_{G})_{\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}}

is the sum-of-entries function.

This procedure can be seen as assigning a linear map
(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes\ell}
to a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(\ell,k)
and an unlabelled graph
G
. Moreover, one may note the following. Consider an
(\ell,k)
-partition
P=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
. Construct a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{P}\eqqcolon\Gamma(P)
by setting
V(\mathbf{P})=\{v_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
and
E(\mathbf{P})=\varnothing
, as well as
l_{in}(v_{i})=\{i\mid i\in[\ell],i_{U}\in p_{i}\}
and
l_{out}(v_{i})=\{i\mid i\in[k],i_{L}\in p_{i}\}
.
Then it is a routine calculation to verify that
\Gamma(P\otimes P^{\prime})=\Gamma(P)\otimes\Gamma(P^{\prime})
,
\Gamma(P\circ P^{\prime})=\Gamma(P)\circ\Gamma(P^{\prime})
, and
\Gamma(P^{*})=\Gamma(P)^{*}
. Furthermore,
\Gamma
has an inverse that assigns to an edgeless bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
the partition
P_{\mathbf{K}}=\Gamma^{-1}(\mathbf{K})=\{\{i_{U}\mid i\in l_{in}(v)\}\cup\{i_{L}\mid i\in l_{out}(v)\}\mid v\in V(K)\}
.

Example 2.30
.

Consider the edgeless bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}
associated to the single-block partitions
\{\{1_{L},\dots,\ell_{L},1_{U},\dots,k_{U}\}\}
. Then
\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}
is a single-vertex graph with
k
in-labels and
\ell
out-labels. Since
\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}
has no edges,
\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}_{G}
depends only on
\lvert V(G)\rvert\eqqcolon n
and we have

(\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}_{G})_{\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }u_{1}=\dots=u_{\ell}=v_{1}=\dots=v_{k},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular, we have
\mathbf{M}^{\ell,k}_{G}=M^{(n),\ell,k}
for all
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
. An important special case is
\mathbf{M}^{1,1}_{G}
, which is just the identity matrix
I_{n}
. We thus also write
\mathbf{I}
for
\mathbf{M}^{1,1}
.

Example 2.31
.

Consider the
swap graph

\mathbf{S}
associated to the swap partition

, illustrated in
Figure

6
. Again, since
\mathbf{S}
does not have any edges,
\mathbf{S}_{G}
only depends on
n
, and we have

(\mathbf{S}_{G})_{u_{1}u_{2},v_{1}v_{2}}=\delta_{u_{1}v_{2}}\delta_{u_{2}v_{1}}.

As a linear map, this correponds exactly to
S^{(n)}
.

The reader may convince themself that every partition can be constructed from single block partitions and the swap map using composition and tensor products. Examples
2.30
and
2.31
thus imply that
\mathbf{K}_{G}=T_{\Gamma^{-1}(\mathbf{K})}
for all edgeless bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{K}
.

\mathbf{A}
\mathbf{I}
\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
\mathbf{S}

Figure 6:
Some important bilabelled graphs.

Generally, of course, bilabelled graphs can have edges. An important example is the
bilabelled edge
.

Example 2.32
.

The bilabelled edge is given by
\mathbf{A}=(K_{2},(v_{1}),(v_{2}))
. For any graph
G
, we have

(\mathbf{A}_{G})_{vw}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }vw\in E(G),\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In other words,
\mathbf{A}_{G}
is just the adjacency matrix
A_{G}
of
G
.

For arbitrary bilabelled graphs, Mančinska and Roberson
[
14
]
proved an important generalisation of
Proposition

2.18
.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}
. Then we have
(\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{K^{\prime}})_{G}=\mathbf{K}_{G}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{\prime}_{G}
,
(\mathbf{K}^{*})_{G}=\mathbf{K}_{G}^{*}
, and if
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
are compatible,
(\mathbf{K}\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime})_{G}=\mathbf{K}_{G}\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime}_{G}
.

Based on this result, they introduced
graph categories
, which give rise to concrete tensor categories with duals.

Definition 2.34
.

A
graph category

\mathcal{F}
is a collection of bilabelled graphs, such that

1.

if
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(r,s)
, then
\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(k+r,\ell+s)
,

2.

if
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,r)
, then
\mathbf{K}\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(k,r)
,

3.

if
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)
then
\mathbf{K}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)
,

4.

\mathbf{I}\in\mathcal{F}(1,1)
,

5.

\mathbf{M}^{2,0}\in\mathcal{F}(2,0)
,

where
\mathcal{F}(k,\ell)=\mathcal{F}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
.

Just as for partition categories, we write
\langle{}\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{M}_{n}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
to denote the graph category generated by the bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots\mathbf{M}_{n}
, that is, the closure of
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{M}_{n}
under tensor products, composition, and taking adjoints.
Lemma

2.33
now implies that for every graph category
\mathcal{F}
and graph
G
,

C(\ell,k)=\lspan\{\mathbf{K}_{G}\mid G\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)\}

is a concrete tensor category with duals. In particular, every pair of a graph category
\mathcal{F}
and a graph
G
corresponds to a unique compact matrix quantum group through Woronowicz’ Tannaka-Krein duality (
Theorem

2.11
). Mančinska and Roberson call these quantum groups
graph-theoretical
.
An important example is the
quantum automorphism group
of a graph.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
G
be a graph. The quantum automorphism group
\Qut(G)
of
G
is the unique compact matrix quantum group with intertwiners

C^{\Qut(G)}=\lspan\{T_{K}\mid K\in\langle M^{1,0},M^{1,2},A_{G}\rangle\}.

In other words, the quantum automorphism group
\Qut(G)
is obtained by starting with the quantum symmetric group
S_{n}^{+}
, and adding the adjacency matrix of
G
as an intertwiner. In particular, by Examples
2.30
and
2.32
, it can be phrased entirely in terms of homomorphism tensors: It is the graph-theoretical quantum group obtained by starting with the graph category underlying
S_{n}^{+}
, and adding the bilabelled edge
\mathbf{A}
.
Based on these observations, Mančinska and Roberson eventually prove the following result.

Theorem 2.36

(Theorem 7.16 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
G
and
H
be graphs. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

There exists a quantum permutation matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, that is,
G
and
H
are quantum isomorphic.

2.

There exists a weak isomorphism between
\Qut(G)
and
\Qut(H)
.

3.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the
(0,0)
-bilabelled graphs in
\langle{}\mathbf{M}^{1,0},\mathbf{M}^{1,2},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
, which are precisely the planar graphs.

A
quantum permutation matrix
is a matrix in
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
for some C
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}
, satisfying the generating relations of the quantum symmetric group in
Equation

2
. Generally, we will call matrices with entries in a C
∗
-algebra
quantum matrices
. A
weak isomorphism
is a notion introduced to capture that the intertwiner spaces of two quantum groups are essentially the same, cf.
[
14
, Definition 7.2]
and later
Definition

3.40
.

3
Counting Homomorphisms from Arbitrary Intertwiner Graphs

The idea of adding the adjacency matrix of a graph to the intertwiners of an easy quantum group
Q
is of course not unique to the quantum symmetric group. This procedure gives rise to a class of “graph-dependent” quantum subgroups of
Q
, generalising the concept of quantum automorphism groups. The question thus arises whether
Theorem

2.36
can also be generalised in this way, replacing the
\Qut(G)
and
\Qut(H)
with the corresponding subgroups of
Q
, the matrix
\mathcal{U}
with a matrix satisfying the same intertwiner relations as the fundamental representation of
Q
, and the graph class with the bilabelled graphs corresponding to the intertwiners of
Q
.

3.1
The Graph Instatiation

Let us begin by formalising these ideas. The first is the idea of “adding the bilabelled edge”. For brevity, we simplify some definitions. Let
Q
be an orthogonal easy quantum group corresponding to some partition category
\langle P_{1},\dots,P_{k}\rangle
. Then we call the linear maps
I,M^{2,0},M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
corresponding to
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 62.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}},\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 36.22 M 0 36.22 L 39.37 36.22 M 39.37 36.22 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}},P_{1},\dots,P_{k}
the
generators
of
Q
. Moreover, we denote the corresponding edgeless bilabelled graphs by bold letters, in this case
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{M}_{k}
.

Definition 3.37
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group with generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
and let
G
be a graph. Then the
G
-instantiation

Q(G)
of
Q
is the unique compact matrix quantum group with intertwiners
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell)\coloneqq\lspan\{\mathbf{K}_{G}\mid\mathbf{K}\in\langle{}\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{M}_{k},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)\}
.

In particular, the
G
-instantiation of
S^{+}_{n}
is simply the quantum automorphism group
\Qut(G)
, and we have
C^{S^{+}_{n}}_{G}=C^{\Qut(G)}
.

Definition 3.38
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group with generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
. We call the set of graphs
\mathcal{F}=\langle{}\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{M}_{k},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
the
intertwiner graphs
of
Q
. Moreover, for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)\subseteq\mathcal{F}
the set of
(\ell,k)
-bilabelled graphs in
\mathcal{F}
.

For example, Mančinska and Roberson showed that the
(0,0)
-bilabelled graphs among the intertwiner graphs of
S_{n}^{+}
are precisely the planar graphs.
Next, we define a shorthand notation for homomorphism indistinguishability over the
(0,0)
-bilabelled graphs in a graph category.

Definition 3.39
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a collection of bilabelled graphs. We say that two graphs
G
,
H
are
\mathcal{F}
-isomorphic
, in symbols
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
, if
G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
, that is,

\hom(F,G)=\hom(F,H)

for all
F\in\mathcal{F}(0,0)
.

Central to Mančinska and Roberson’s proof is the concept of a weak isomorphism between quantum automorphism groups. Their definition can be generalised to graph instantiations of arbitrary easy quantum groups in a straightforward way.

Definition 3.40
.

Let
Q=(C(Q),\mathcal{U},n)
be an easy quantum group with generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
, and let
G
,
H
be two graphs of order
n
.
A
weak isomorphism
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
is a bijection
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
such that for all
T,T^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}
,

1.

\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}
and
\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
,

2.

\Phi(TT^{\prime})=\Phi(T)\Phi(T^{\prime})
if
T,T^{\prime}
are compatible,

3.

\Phi(T\otimes T^{\prime})=\Phi(T)\otimes\Phi(T^{\prime})
,

4.

\Phi(T^{*})=\Phi(T)^{*}
,

5.

For all
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
, the restriction of
\Phi
to
C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
is a bijective linear map to
C^{Q}_{H}(\ell,k)
.

These functions essentially capture that the intertwiner spaces of the two graph instantiations have the same structure. In particular, every construction of an intertwiner from the generators of
Q(G)
can be mirrored in
Q(H)
.

Remark 3.41
.

Technically, we should write
\Phi_{M_{1},\dots,M_{k}}
since the definition depends on the choice of generators. However, it will later become clear that for any two choices of generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
and
M^{\prime}_{1},\dots,M^{\prime}_{\ell}
we have that
\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
implies
\Phi(M^{\prime}_{j})=M^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in[\ell]
.

In the next two sections, we will prove the following theorem, which is exactly our desired generalisation of
Theorem

2.36
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
Q
be an orthogonal easy quantum group with generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{m}
and let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of its intertwiner graphs. Let
G
and
H
be graphs. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
.

2.

There exists a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
.

3.

There exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M=M\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for all
M\in C^{Q}(k,\ell)
, such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

4.

There exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M_{i}=M_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for all
i\in[m]
and
M_{i}\in C^{Q}(\ell,k)
, such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

Moreover, if these statements hold, then

5.

Q(G)
and
Q(H)
are unitarily monoidally equivalent.

A
quantum orthogonal matrix
is a quantum matrix satisfying the definining relations of the quantum orthogonal group in
Equation

1
.
The proof of the implications 4
\Rightarrow
2
\Rightarrow
1 is more or less an obvious generalisation of Mančinska and Roberson’s original proof using our just established definitions. We nevertheless include the proofs for the sake of completeness. The converse direction is much more involved.
The original proof uses results on quantum group orbits and orbitals that do not generalise beyond the quantum symmetric group. Here we take a different approach, using previous results on monoidal categories, or, more precisely, C
∗
-tensor categories. This approach also yields statement 5, which was implicitly conjectured in
[
14
]
.

We split the proof into two sections. In the remainder of this section, we examine the connection between homomorphism indistinguishability and the structure of intertwiner spaces through weak isomorphisms. We show that graphs
G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the unlabelled fragments of the intertwiner graphs of an easy quantum group
Q
if and only if there exists a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
, hence proving 1
\Leftrightarrow
2.

In the next section, we want to show that the existence of a weak isomorphism is equivalent to a statement that more closely resembles the formulation of quantum isomorphism as a C
∗
-valued linear system of equations
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.
To that end, we first show 4
\Rightarrow
2, proving that any quantum matrix satisfying the intertwiner relations of the underlying easy quantum group induces a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
. Since the implication 3
\Rightarrow
4 is trivial, we conclude by proving that 2
\Rightarrow
3 using category-theoretic machinery in the form of a theorem by Neshveyev and Tuset
[
16
]
. On the way, we obtain 2
\Rightarrow
5.

3.2
\mathcal{F}
-isomorphism implies weak isomorphism of graph instantiations

Suppose then that
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
. We want to show that there exists a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
. The first requirement is that
G
and
H
must be of the same order, which follows without much difficulty.

Observation 3.42
.

Let
Q
be a compact matrix quantum group and
\mathcal{F}
the class of its intertwiner graphs. Then for all graphs
G
,
H
with
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
it holds that
\lvert G\rvert=\lvert H\rvert
.

Proof 3.43
.

Note that
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
contains the single vertex graph
K_{1}
. Indeed, we have
K_{1}=\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
. The result now follows from the fact that
\hom(K_{1},G)=\lvert G\rvert
for all graphs
G
.

The central observation is now that for any graphs
G
and
H
,
C^{Q}_{G}
and
C^{Q}_{H}
already look quite similar on the surface, since the intertwiner spaces are generated by the homomorphism tensors of the same bilabelled graphs. In particular, the individual vector spaces
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell)
and
C^{Q}_{H}(k,\ell)
are essentially spanned by the “same” vectors. Now if the scalar product between any two vectors in
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell)
always equals that of the corresponding vectors in
C^{Q}_{H}(k,\ell)
, then we can conclude by a Gram-Schmidt argument that there exists a unitary linear map between the two vector spaces that maps a vector in
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell)
to its analogue in
C^{Q}_{H}(k,\ell)
. This is formalised in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.44

(Lemma 2.4.3 in
[
21
]
)
.

Let
V
and
W
be finite-dimensional complex vector spaces.
Let
I
be a possibly infinite set.
Let
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(w_{i})_{i\in I}
be two sequences of vectors such that
v_{i}\in V
and
w_{i}\in W
for all
i\in I
.
Suppose that

1.

the
v_{i}
for
i\in I
span
V
, the
w_{i}
for
i\in I
span
W
, and

2.

\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=\langle w_{i},w_{j}\rangle
for all
i,j\in I
.

Then there exists a unitary linear map
\Phi\colon V\to W
such that
\Phi(v_{i})=w_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

The next lemma further implies that the scalar product of vectors in
C^{Q}_{G}
and
C^{Q}_{H}
is precisely given by homomorphism counts of unlabelled intertwiner graphs of
Q
.

Lemma 3.45
.

Let
Q
be a compact matrix quantum group. For all
k,\ell
, there exists a scalar product on
C^{Q}(k,\ell)
defined by

\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle\coloneqq(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T_{1}^{*}T_{2})(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}

for all
T_{1},T_{2}\in C^{Q}(k,\ell)
.

Proof 3.46
.

We show that
\langle\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}},\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\rangle
is indeed a scalar product. Let
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\in C^{Q}(k,\ell)
. It is a straightforward check of dimensions to see that
\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}
, so it remains to show that

1.

\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle^{*}=\langle T_{2},T_{1}\rangle

2.

\langle T_{1},aT_{2}+bT_{3}\rangle=a\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle+b\langle T_{1},T_{3}\rangle
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{C}

3.

\langle T_{1},T_{1}\rangle\geq 0

We have

\displaystyle\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle^{*}

\displaystyle=\left[(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T_{1}^{*}T_{2})(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}\right]^{*}

\displaystyle=(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T_{1}^{*}T_{2})^{*}(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}((I^{\otimes k})^{*}\otimes(T_{1}^{*}T_{2})^{*})(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T_{2}^{*}T_{1})(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\langle T_{2},T_{1}\rangle,

while (b) follows from the linearity of matrix multiplication and the tensor product. To see that (c) holds, observe that

\displaystyle\langle T,T\rangle

\displaystyle=(M^{0,2})^{\otimes k}(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T^{*}T)(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\left[(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T)(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}\right]^{*}\left[(I^{\otimes k}\otimes T)(M^{2,0})^{\otimes k}\right]

\displaystyle\eqqcolon v^{*}v,

where
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{k+\ell}}
, and thus
v^{*}v\geq 0
.

Since the construction only involves the intertwiners
M^{2,0}
and
I
, which correspond to bilabelled graphs, we can extend
\langle\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}},\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\rangle
to
(k,\ell)
-bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}
for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle}
is an unlabelled graph, and we have
\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle}_{G}=(\langle\mathbf{K}_{G},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G}\rangle)\in C^{Q}_{G}(0,0)
. We can now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.47
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group,
\mathcal{F}
the set of its intertwiner graphs, and let
G
and
H
be graphs such that
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
. Then there exists a weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
.

Proof 3.48
.

First note that by
3.42
, we have
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell),C^{Q}_{H}(k,\ell)\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}\times n^{\ell}}
for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
We now define sequences
(v_{\mathbf{K}})_{\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}}\subseteq C^{Q}_{G}
,
(w_{\mathbf{K}})_{\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}}\subseteq C^{Q}_{H}
by setting
v_{\mathbf{K}}\coloneqq\mathbf{K}_{G}
and
w_{\mathbf{K}}\coloneqq\mathbf{K}_{H}
. Then
\{v_{\mathbf{K}}\mid\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)\}
spans
C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
and
\{w_{\mathbf{K}}\mid\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)\}
spans
C^{Q}_{H}(\ell,k)
. Moreover, we have for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell,k)

\displaystyle\langle v_{\mathbf{K}},v_{\mathbf{K^{\prime}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\soe(\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle}_{G})

\displaystyle=\hom(\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle},G)

\displaystyle=\hom(\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle},H)

\displaystyle=\soe(\mathbf{\langle K,K^{\prime}\rangle}_{H})

\displaystyle=\langle w_{\mathbf{K}},w_{\mathbf{K^{\prime}}}\rangle,

where
\langle\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}},\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\rangle
is the scalar product defined in
Lemma

3.45
. Consequently, by
Lemma

3.44
there exists, for all
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
, a unitary linear map
\Phi_{\ell,k}\colon C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)\to C^{Q}_{H}(\ell,k)
, mapping
v_{\mathbf{K}}
to
w_{\mathbf{K}}
for all
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Claim 2
.

\Phi\coloneqq\bigcup_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{\ell,k}
is a weak isomorphism
C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
.

Proof 3.49

(Proof)
.

By linearity, it suffices to show that for all
\mathbf{K}_{G},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G}\in C^{Q}_{G}
with
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}
it holds that

1.

\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}
and
\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
,

2.

\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G}\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G})=\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})\Phi(\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G})
if
\mathbf{K}_{G},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G}
are compatible,

3.

\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G}\otimes\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G})=\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})\otimes\Phi(\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G})
,

4.

\Phi(\mathbf{K}^{*}_{G})=\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})^{*}
,

5.

For all
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
, the restriction of
\Phi
to
C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
is a bijective linear map to
C^{Q}_{H}(\ell,k)
.

First note that the restriction of
\Phi
to
C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
is just
\Phi_{\ell,k}
, which is a unitary linear map and thus bijective. Moreover, we have
A_{G}=\mathbf{A}_{G}=v_{A}
and
A_{H}=\mathbf{A}_{H}=w_{A}
and thus
\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}
; as well as
M_{i}=v_{\mathbf{M}_{i}}=w_{\mathbf{M}_{i}}
(since
\mathbf{M}_{i}
is edgeless and
G
,
H
have the same order) and hence
\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}
. To see that (b) holds, observe that for compatible
\mathbf{K}_{G},\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G}\in C^{Q}_{G}
,

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G}\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G})

\displaystyle=\Phi((\mathbf{KK^{\prime}})_{G})=\Phi(v_{\mathbf{KK^{\prime}}})=w_{\mathbf{KK^{\prime}}}=(\mathbf{KK^{\prime}})_{H}=\mathbf{K}_{H}\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{H}=w_{\mathbf{K}}w_{\mathbf{K^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})\Phi(\mathbf{K^{\prime}}_{G}).

The proof of (c) is analogous. Finally, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G}^{*})=\Phi((\mathbf{K}^{*})_{G})=\Phi(v_{\mathbf{K}^{*}})=w_{\mathbf{K}^{*}}=(\mathbf{K}^{*})_{H}=\mathbf{K}_{H}^{*}=\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})^{*},

so (d) is also satisfied, and
\Phi
is a weak isomorphism.

This concludes the proof.

3.3
Weak isomorphism of graph instantiations implies
\mathcal{F}
-isomorphism

The converse direction is a direct generalisation of Mančinska and Roberson’s proof. The main observation is again that a weak isomorphism mirrors the construction of intertwiners using composition, tensor product, and adjoints. In particular, the following holds.

Lemma 3.50
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group,
\mathcal{F}
the set of its intertwiner graphs, and
G
,
H
be graphs such that there exists a weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
. Then for all
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}
it holds that
\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})=\mathbf{K}_{H}
.

Proof 3.51
.

Since
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists a construction of
\mathbf{K}
from generators
\mathbf{M}_{1},\dots,\allowbreak\mathbf{M}_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbf{A}
using the operations
\circ,\otimes,*
. We fix such a construction and proceed inductively.
Per definition of weak isomorphisms, we have
\Phi((\mathbf{M}_{i})_{G})=\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}=(\mathbf{M}_{i})_{H}
for all
i\in[k]
and
\Phi(\mathbf{A}_{G})=\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}=\mathbf{A}_{H}
.
Now suppose
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\circ\mathbf{K}_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{K}_{G})

\displaystyle=\Phi((\mathbf{K}_{1})_{G}(\mathbf{K}_{2})_{G})=\Phi((\mathbf{K}_{1})_{G})\Phi((\mathbf{K}_{2})_{G})=(\mathbf{K}_{1})_{H}(\mathbf{K}_{2})_{H}=(\mathbf{K}_{1}\mathbf{K}_{2})_{H}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{H}.

The cases
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{2}
and
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}^{*}
are analogous.

For easy quantum groups, we can always assume that
M^{2,0}
is among the generators. This allows us to determine the
(0,0)
-slice of any weak isomorphism.

Lemma 3.52
.

Let
(C(Q),\mathcal{U},n)
be an easy quantum group, and
G
,
H
be graphs that admit a weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
. Then
\Phi(I^{\otimes 0})=I^{\otimes 0}
. In particular, the restriction of
\Phi
to
C^{Q}_{G}(0,0)
is the identity map.

Proof 3.53
.

Per definition of weak isomorphisms, we have
\lvert G\rvert=\lvert H\rvert\eqqcolon n
. We use that the map
M\coloneqq M^{2,0}\in C^{Q}_{G}
satifies
M^{*}M=nI^{\otimes 0}
. Then we get

n\Phi(I^{\otimes 0})=\Phi(nI^{\otimes 0})=\Phi(M^{*}M)=\Phi(M^{*})\Phi(M)=M^{*}M=nI^{\otimes 0}.

Dividing by
n
on both sides gives us the desired result. The linearity of
\Phi
then further implies that its restriction to
C^{Q}_{G}(0,0)
must be the identity map.

This allow us to prove implication 2
\Rightarrow
1 of
1
.

Lemma 3.54
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group,
\mathcal{F}
the set of its intertwiner graphs, and
G
and
H
be graphs that admit a weak isomorphism
\Phi
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
. Then
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
.

Proof 3.55
.

By
Lemma

3.52
, the weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
is the identity on
C^{Q}_{G}(0,0)
. Now let
\mathbf{F}=(F,(),())\in\mathcal{F}(0,0)
be arbitrary. Then we have
\mathbf{F}_{G}\in C^{Q}_{G}(0,0)
and thus

\hom(F,G)=\soe(\mathbf{F}_{G})=\soe(\Phi(\mathbf{F}_{G}))=\soe(\mathbf{F}_{H})=\hom(F,H).

4
Monoidal Equivalence and Intertwining Quantum Matrices

So far we have seen that the characterisation of intertwiners as homomorphism tensors implies that homomorphism indistinguishability of graphs
G
and
H
over the unlabelled intertwiner graphs of an easy quantum group
Q
means that the intertwiner spaces of the graph instantiations
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
“look the same”. While this is already an interesting result, we would like to obtain a characterisation of
G
and
H
in terms of a C
∗
-algebra-valued linear system, analogous to quantum isomorphism.
In this section we derive such a characterisation, proving the equivalences 2
\Leftrightarrow
3
\Leftrightarrow
4, as well as
2\Rightarrow 5
of
1
.

4.1
Quantum Matrices induce Weak Isomorphisms

The first direction is again a straightforward generalisation of Mančinska and Roberson’s proof that we include for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 4.56
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group with generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{m}
and let
G
and
H
be graphs. If there exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M_{i}=M_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for all
i\in[m]
and
M_{i}\in C^{Q}(k,\ell)
, then there exists a weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
.

Proof 4.57
.

We show that the map
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
given by
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T=\Phi(T)\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
for
T\in C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
is a weak isomorphism.
To see that
\Phi
is well-defined, note that
T\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
implies that
T=T^{\prime}
, since
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
is orthogonal and thus invertible.

Now consider the generators
M_{1},\dots,M_{k}
of
Q
. Per assumption
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}M_{i}=M_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
for all
i\in[k]
with
M_{i}\in C^{Q}(\ell,k)
and
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, so we have
\Phi(M_{i})=M_{i}
and
\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}
. Since the
M_{i}
and
A_{G}
generate
C^{Q}_{G}
, to prove that
\Phi
is defined everywhere on
C^{Q}_{G}
, it suffices to show that
\Phi
is linear and commutes with tensor product, composition and hermitian adjoint.

Suppose that
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T_{i}=T_{i}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
and
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
for
i\in[m]
. Then it is straightforward to see that

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}T_{i}\right)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}T^{\prime}_{i}\right)\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}.

Now let
T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k),T_{2},T_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}(k,m)
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T_{1}=T_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T_{2}=T_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes m}
. Then

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}(T_{1}T_{2})=T_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T_{2}=T_{1}^{\prime}T_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes m}.

Next let
T_{1},T_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k),T_{2},T_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}(r,s)
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T_{1}=T_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes r}T_{2}=T_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes s}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(\ell+r)}(T_{1}\otimes T_{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\otimes r})(T_{1}\otimes T_{2})=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T_{1}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\otimes r}T_{2}

\displaystyle=T_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}\otimes T_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes s}=(T_{1}^{\prime}\otimes T_{2}^{\prime})\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(k+s)}

Finally, let
T,T^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}[T^{*}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell})^{*}]\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}[\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}T]^{*}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}[T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}]^{*}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k})^{*}T^{\prime*}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}=T^{\prime*}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}.

We thus have shown that
\Phi
is linear and commutes with the necessary operations. Since
M_{1},\dots,M_{k},A_{H}
generate
C^{Q}_{H}
and these intertwiners are in the image of
\Phi
, it follows that
\Phi
is surjective. To see that
\Phi
is injective, suppose that
\Phi(T)=\Phi(T^{\prime})
. Then
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T=\Phi(T)\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}=\Phi(T^{\prime})\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T^{\prime}
, thus the orthogonality of
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
implies that
T=T^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\Phi
is a weak isomorphism, which completes the proof.

4.2
A brief excursion into Category Theory

In order to prove the converse direction, we need to introduce a few more concepts from category theory. In the following let
Q
be a compact matrix quantum group and
G
,
H
be graphs. Our first goal is to show that a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
is really just a
monoidal equivalence
of
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
in disguise.

Recall the definition of the representation category
\Rep(Q)
from
Section

2.2
. It is known that
\Rep(Q)
forms a so-called
C
∗
-tensor category
. For a full definition of C
∗
-tensor categories see e.g. Definition 2.3.13 in
[
13
]
1
1

1

For simplicity and ease of notation, we tacitly assume that every C
∗
-tensor category is
strict
, i.e. that the associator
\alpha
as well as the left and right unitors
\lambda
and
\rho
are the identity. This does not change the validity of the results, since
\Rep(Q)
is strict for all compact quantum groups
Q
.
—for our purposes it suffices to know that they are monoidal categories that allow to take linear combinations and adjoints of morphisms.

Definition 4.58
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be C
∗
-tensor categories. A
tensor functor

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
that is linear on morphisms, together with natural isomorphisms

F_{2}\colon F(U)\otimes F(V)\to F(U\otimes V)

such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F(U)\otimes F(V))\otimes F(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(U\otimes V)\otimes F(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F((U\otimes V)\otimes W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(U)\otimes(F(V)\otimes F(W))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(U)\otimes F(V\otimes W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(U\otimes(V\otimes W))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}

commutes, and
F(\mathbf{1})\cong\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
. We call
F

unitary
if additionally
F(g^{*})=F(g)^{*}
for all morphisms
g
and
F_{2}
is unitary.

Definition 4.59
.

We call two compact quantum groups
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}

(unitarily) monoidally equivalent
if there exists a (unitary) tensor functor
\Rep(Q_{1})\to\Rep(Q_{2})
that is fully faithful and essentially surjective.

Now suppose we have a weak isomorphism
\Phi\colon C^{Q}_{G}\to C^{Q}_{H}
. Recall that the sets
C^{Q}_{G}
and
C^{Q}_{H}
comprise the morphisms of the reduced representation categories
\Rep_{0}(Q(G))
and
\Rep_{0}(Q(H))
respectively. The objects of these categories are the tensor powers of the fundamental representations
\mathcal{U}_{G}
of
Q(G)
and
\mathcal{U}_{H}
of
Q(H)
, so given
\Phi
we can construct a functor
F\colon\Rep_{0}(Q(G))\to\Rep_{0}(Q(H))
with
F(\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes k})=\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\otimes k}
and
F(T)=\Phi(T)
for all
T\in\Mor(\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes k},\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes\ell})
and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will show that we can extend this functor from
\Rep_{0}
to the category
\Rep
. The main observation is that for any compact matrix quantum group
Q
, the category
\Rep(Q)
is the pseudoabelian completion of
\Rep_{0}(Q)
. Roughly speaking, in a pseudoabelian category every idempotent endomorphism decomposes into the direct sum of kernel and image. Now for compact matrix quantum groups, every finite-dimensional representation is a direct summand of a tensor power of the fundamental representation.
Moreover, the projectors onto the corresponding subspaces are exactly the idempotent endomorphisms. Consequently, moving to the pseudoabelian completion of the reduced representation category recovers the other finite-dimensional representations as the images of the projectors.

Definition 4.60
.

The
pseudoabelian completion
of a category
\mathcal{C}
is defined as the category given by

Objects:

(A,e)
,
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
,
e\in\Mor(A,A)
idempotent.

Morphisms:

\operatorname{Mor}((A,e),(B,f))=f\Mor(A,B)e
.

Composition:

induced by the composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

Intuitively, the object
(A,e)
corresponds to the “subspace” of
A
that
e
projects onto.
Concretely, this means that for
Q=(C(Q),\mathcal{U},n)
, we can assume
\Rep(Q)
to be of the following form.

Objects:

(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k},T)
,
T\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}\to(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}
linear and idempotent,

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T=T\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Morphisms:

\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k},T),(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell},T^{\prime}))=T^{\prime}ST
,

S\in\Mor(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell})=C(k,\ell)
.

Composition:

natural composition.

This allows us to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.61
.

Let
Q
be a compact matrix quantum group with generators
M_{i}\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{i}}_{G},I^{\otimes k_{i}}),(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell_{i}},I^{\otimes\ell_{i}}))
,
m,k_{i},\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N},i\in[m]
, and let
G
,
H
be graphs that admit a weak isomorphism
\Phi
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
. Then there exists a unitary tensor functor
F\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
that is fully faithful and essentially surjective, such that

1.

F(A_{G})=A_{H}
,

2.

F(M_{i})=M_{i}
for all
i\in[m]
,

3.

F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I^{\otimes k})=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},I^{\otimes k})
,

4.

F_{2}
is the identity.

In particular,
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
are unitarily monoidally equivalent.

Proof 4.62
.

We construct a functor
F\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
as follows.

On objects:

(\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes k},T)\mapsto(\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\otimes k},\Phi(T))
.

On morphisms:

T^{\prime}ST\mapsto\Phi(T^{\prime})\Phi(S)\Phi(T)
,

for
T^{\prime}ST\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes k},T),(\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes\ell},T^{\prime}))
.

To prove that this is well defined, we need to show that
T\in\Mor_{\Rep_{0}(Q(G))}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k})
is idempotent if and only if
\Phi(T)
is idempotent. If
T
is idempotent, we have
\Phi(T)\Phi(T)=\Phi(TT)=\Phi(T)
, so
\Phi(T)
is idempotent.
For the converse suppose that
\Phi(T)
is idempotent. Then
\Phi(TT)=\Phi(T)\Phi(T)=\Phi(T)
and hence
T=TT
by injectivity of
\Phi
.

To see that
F
is a valid functor
\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
, we need to show that
F(\id_{U})=\id_{F(U)}
for all objects
U
and that
F(g\circ f)=F(g)\circ F(f)
for all morphisms
G
,
H
. The identity of
U=(\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\otimes k},T)
is
T
, so we have
F(T)=\Phi(T)=\id_{F(U)}
. Moreover, for morphisms

T_{2}ST_{1}\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{1}},T_{1}),(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{2}},T_{2}))
,

T_{3}S^{\prime}T_{2}\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{2}},T_{2}),(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{3}},T_{3}))

we have

\displaystyle F(T_{2}ST_{1}\circ T_{3}S^{\prime}T_{2})

\displaystyle=\Phi(T_{2}ST_{1}\circ T_{3}S^{\prime}T_{2})

\displaystyle=\Phi(T_{2}ST_{1})\circ\Phi(T_{3}S^{\prime}T_{2})

\displaystyle=\Phi(T_{2})\Phi(S)\Phi(T_{1})\circ\Phi(T_{3})\Phi(S^{\prime})\Phi(T_{2})

\displaystyle=F(T_{2}ST_{1})\circ F(T_{3}S^{\prime}T_{2})

Claim 3
.

F
is a unitary tensor functor.

Proof 4.63

(Proof)
.

It is not hard to see that
F
is linear on morphisms. This follows almost immediately from the linearity of
\Phi
and the fact that it commutes with composition. Moreover, the identities at each object indeed form a natural isomorphism
F_{2}\colon F(U)\otimes F(V)\to F(U\otimes V)
: For objects
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},T)
and
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G},T^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},T)\otimes F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G},T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},\Phi(T))\otimes(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{H},\Phi(T^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{H},\Phi(T)\otimes\Phi(T^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(k+\ell)}_{H},\Phi(T\otimes T^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(k+\ell)}_{G},T\otimes T^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G},T\otimes T^{\prime}).

where the second equality uses the natural tensor structure on
\Rep(Q(H))
. For morphisms
T_{2}ST_{1}\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{1}},T_{1}),(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{2}},T_{2})),T_{4}S^{\prime}T_{3}\in\Mor((\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{3}},T_{2}),

(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{4}},T_{3}))
, we have

\displaystyle F(T_{2}ST_{1}\otimes T_{4}S^{\prime}T_{3})

\displaystyle=\Phi(T_{2}ST_{1}\otimes T_{4}S^{\prime}T_{3})=\Phi(T_{2}ST_{1})\otimes\Phi(T_{4}S^{\prime}T_{3})

\displaystyle=F(T_{2}ST_{1})\otimes F(T_{4}S^{\prime}T_{3}).

The unit
\mathbf{1}_{G}
is the object
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}_{G},I^{\otimes 0})
which, by
Lemma

3.52
, gets mapped to
F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}_{G},I^{\otimes 0})=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}_{H},\Phi(I^{\otimes 0}))=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}_{H},I^{\otimes 0})
, which is the unit
\mathbf{1}_{H}
in
\Rep(Q(H))
. In particular,
F(\mathbf{1}_{G})\cong\mathbf{1}_{H}
.
Finally, the unitarity of
F
follows immediately from the fact that
\Phi
commutes with the hermitian adjoint.

Claim 4
.

F
is fully faithful and essentially surjective.

Proof 4.64

(Proof)
.

We have already seen that
\Phi
is bijective on the idempotent intertwiners. This implies that
F
is essentially surjective, i.e. surjective on objects. Indeed, suppose
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},T)\in\Rep(Q(H))
. Since
T\in C^{Q}_{G}(k,k)
is idempotent, there must exist an idempotent
T^{\prime}\in C^{Q}_{H}(k,k)
such that
\Phi(T^{\prime})=T
, and thus
F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},T^{\prime})=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},\Phi(T^{\prime}))=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},T)
.

That
F
is fully faithful, i.e. bijective on morphisms, follows immediately from the fact that the restrictions of
\Phi
to
C^{Q}_{G}(k,\ell)
are bijective for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

We have thus shown that there exists a unitary tensor functor
F\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
that is fully faithful and essentially surjective. Per definition, it follows that
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
are unitarily monoidally equivalent. Conditions 1, 2, 3 follow immediately from the construction of
F
.

In this sense, our weak isomorphisms serve as a combinatorial view on the category-theoretic structure of representation categories.

4.3
Weak Isomorphisms induce Quantum Matrices

We have seen that a weak isomorphism between two graph instatiations
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
witnesses that
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
are monoidally equivalent.
The central result that will allow us to prove the converse of
Lemma

4.56
is that any two monoidally equivalent C
∗
-tensor categories admit a
linking algebra
that is compatible with the morphisms in a specific sense. This linking algebra will provide the entries for our desired quantum matrix.
We first need to clarify the dual structure of the representation categories. Recall the following definition of dual objects.

Definition 4.65
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a C
∗
-tensor category. An object
\bar{U}
is called the
dual
of an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
if there exists morphisms
R\colon\mathbf{1}\to\bar{U}\otimes U
and
\bar{R}\colon\mathbf{1}\to U\otimes\bar{U}
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\otimes\bar{U}\otimes U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{R}^{*}\otimes\id}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{U}\otimes U\otimes\bar{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes\bar{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{*}\otimes\id}

are the identity morphisms. We call these conditions the
conjugate equations
.

Definition 4.66
.

A
rigid C
∗
-tensor category
is a C
∗
-tensor category where every element has a dual.

An important result is that for every compact quantum group
Q
, its representation category
\Rep(Q)
is a rigid C
∗
-tensor category—see for instance
[
16
, Example 2.2.3]
.

Finally, we need to introduce the concept of a
fiber functor
. Essentially, this is a special type of forgetful functor, mapping representations to their underlying spaces. We give a definition for the case of rigid C
∗
-tensor categories.

Definition 4.67
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a rigid C
∗
-tensor category. A
fiber functor
is a tensor functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
, where
\operatorname{fdHilb}
denotes the category of finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

The representation category
\Rep(Q)
of any compact quantum group
Q
admits a canonical fiber functor
F_{Q}\colon\Rep(Q)\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
defined as follows.

On objects:

\mathcal{V}\mapsto\mathbb{C}^{m}
for all representations
\mathcal{V}\in C(Q)^{m\times m}
.

On morphisms:

T\mapsto T
for all intertwiners
T
of
Q
.

Now the following is straightforward to verify.

Lemma 4.68
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
be C
∗
-tensor categories and let
E\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
F\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
be (unitary) tensor functors. Then
F\circ E\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
is also a (unitary) tensor functor.

Corollary 4.69
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group and
G
,
H
be graphs. Suppose
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
are monoidally equivalent, witnessed by a tensor functor
F\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
. Then
F_{Q(H)}\circ F
is a fiber functor
\Rep(Q(G))\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
.

Consequently, the monoidal equivalence of
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
yields another fiber functor
\Rep(Q(G))\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
in addition to the canonical fiber functor
F_{Q(G)}
. This allows us to use the following theorem by Neshveyev and Tuset, which is stated in terms of fiber functors.

Theorem 4.70

(Theorem 2.3.11 in
[
16
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a C
∗
-tensor category with duals and
E,F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
be two fiber functors. Then there exists a C
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}
, and for every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
a unitary element
X^{U}\in\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(E(U),F(U))
such that

1.

If
T\in\Mor(U,V)
, then
(I\otimes F(T))X^{U}=X^{V}(I\otimes E(T))
.

2.

(I\otimes F_{2})X^{U}_{12}X^{V}_{13}=X^{U\otimes V}(I\otimes E_{2})
.

3.

If
(R,\bar{R})
is a solution of the conjugate equations for
U
and
\bar{U}
, then

(X^{U}_{12})^{*}(\mathbf{1}\otimes F_{2}^{*}F(\bar{R}))=X^{\bar{U}}_{13}(\mathbf{1}\otimes E_{2}^{*}E(\bar{R})).

Here
B(E(U),F(U))
denotes the set of bounded linear operators from
E(U)
to
F(U)
. The expression
X_{12}
uses the so-called leg numbering notation, which is used to “pad” operators such that they only act on certain subspaces: If
X=\sum_{i}z_{i}\otimes T_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(E(U),F(U))
, then
X_{12}=\sum_{i}z_{i}\otimes T_{i}\otimes I
and
X_{13}=\sum_{i}z_{i}\otimes I\otimes T_{i}
.

Now if there exists a weak isomorphism between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
,
Lemma

4.61
guarantees the existence of a tensor functor
\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
, which in turn, as we have already noted, yields two fiber functors
\Rep(Q(G))\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
.

Theorem

4.70
thus already looks quite similar to what we want to prove. To bring it into the form that we need, we first note that we already know the solutions to the conjugate equations for
(\mathcal{U},I)\in\Rep(Q(G))
.

Lemma 4.71
.

Let
(C(Q),\mathcal{U},n)
be a compact matrix quantum group. Then
(\mathcal{U},I)
is self-dual in
\Rep(Q)
and
R=\bar{R}=M^{2,0}
solves the conjugate equations.

Proof 4.72
.

First note that
M^{2,0}
is a
(2,0)
-intertwiner, thus in particular a morphism
\mathbf{1}\to(\mathcal{U},I)\otimes(\mathcal{U},I)
in
\Rep(Q)
. The identity at
(\mathcal{U},I)
is
I
, so we have to show that
((M^{2,0})^{*}\otimes I)(I\otimes M^{2,0})=I
. Indeed we have

\displaystyle((M^{2,0})^{*}\otimes I)(I\otimes M^{2,0})

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{i}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes I\big)\big(\sum_{i}I\otimes e_{i}\otimes e_{i}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}e_{j}\otimes e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\delta_{ij}\otimes e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes e_{i}=I.

This allows us to prove the following theorem. For simplicity, we will identify
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I^{\otimes k})\in\Rep(Q(G))
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}
. We can do this without loss of generality, since the subcategory of objects
(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I^{\otimes k})
is just (equal to) the reduced representation category
\Rep_{0}(Q(G))
.

Theorem 4.73
.

Let
(C(Q),\mathcal{U},n)
be an easy quantum group and
G
and
H
be graphs of size
n
that admit a weak isomorphism
\Phi
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
. Then there exists a C
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}
and for all
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}\in\Rep(Q(G))
a unitary matrix
Z^{(k)}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n^{k}\times n^{k}}
, such that for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}

1.

If
T\in\Mor(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G})
, then
Z^{(\ell)}T=\Phi(T)Z^{(k)}
.

2.

Z^{(k)}\otimes Z^{(\ell)}=Z^{(k+\ell)}
.

Moreover, the entries
z_{ij}
of
Z^{(1)}
satisfy
z_{ij}^{*}=z_{ij}
.

Proof 4.74
.

We start with some observations. The existence of a weak isomorphism
\Phi
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
implies by
Lemma

4.61
that there exists a unitary tensor functor
F^{H,G}\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\Rep(Q(H))
such that

1.

F^{H,G}(A_{G})=A_{H}
,

2.

F^{H,G}(M_{i})=M_{i}=\Phi(M_{i})
for all generators
M_{i}
of
Q
,

3.

F^{H,G}_{2}
is the identity,

4.

F^{H,G}(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I)=(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{H},I)
.

By
Corollary

4.69
, there exist two fiber functors
E,F\colon\Rep(Q(G))\to\operatorname{fdHilb}
given by
E=F_{Q(G)}
and
F=F_{Q(H)}\circ F^{H,G}
. By property (d), we have
E(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I)=F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},I)=(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes k}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}}
. This means that we can identify
\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(E(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}),F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}))=\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}},\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}})
with the
n^{k}\times n^{k}
matrices over
\mathcal{Z}
.
We use the symbol
\triangleq
to denote this identification.
In particular, we have for
Z\in\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}},\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}})
,

\displaystyle Z_{12}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}z_{ij}\otimes e_{ij}\otimes I=\left(\sum_{ij}z_{ij}\otimes e_{ij}\right)\otimes I\triangleq Z\otimes I,

(3)

\displaystyle Z_{13}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}z_{ij}\otimes I\otimes e_{ij}\triangleq\sum_{ij}z_{ij}\cdot(\delta_{i_{2}i}\delta_{j_{2}j}\delta_{i_{1}j_{2}})_{i_{1}i_{2},j_{1}j_{2}}=I\otimes Z.

(4)

Since the canonical fiber functors
F_{Q(G)},F_{Q(H)}
are the identity on morphisms, we get that for all
T\in\Mor(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G})
we have
E(T)=T
, and for all generators
M_{i}
of
Q
we have
F(M_{i})=\Phi(M_{i})
. We also get
F(A_{G})=\Phi(A_{G})=A_{H}
. An argument similar to
Lemma

3.50
then shows that
F(T)=\Phi(T)
for all
T\in\Mor(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G},\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G})
.

We can now apply
Theorem

4.70
to the functors
E
and
F
. We let
Z^{(k)}\coloneqq X^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}}
.
Then property 1 of
Theorem

4.70
simplifies under the above observations and the identification of
\mathcal{Z}\otimes B(E(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}),F(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}))
with
\mathcal{Z}^{n^{k}\times n^{k}}
to

\Phi(T)Z^{(k)}=Z^{(\ell)}T,

as desired. By property (c) of
F^{H,G}
, we get that
E_{2}
and
F_{2}
are the identity. Thus, using
3
and
4
, property 2 simplifies to

Z^{(k)}\otimes Z^{(\ell)}=(Z^{(k)}\otimes I)(I\otimes Z^{(\ell)})\triangleq Z^{(k)}_{12}Z^{(\ell)}_{13}\triangleq Z^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}_{G}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}_{G}}=Z^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(k+\ell)}_{G}}=Z^{(k+\ell)}.

It remains to show that the entries
z_{ij}
of
Z^{(1)}
satisfy
z_{ij}^{*}=z_{ij}
. By
Lemma

4.71
,
R=\bar{R}=M^{2,0}
solves the conjugate equations for
\mathcal{U}_{G}\in\Rep(Q(G))
. Thus by property 3 of
Theorem

4.70
, we get

(Z^{(1)}\otimes I)^{*}M^{2,0}=(I\otimes Z^{(1)})M^{2,0},

and we have

\displaystyle(Z^{(1)}\otimes I)^{*}M^{2,0}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{ij}z_{ij}^{*}e_{ji}\otimes I\right)M^{2,0}=\sum_{ijk}z_{ij}^{*}(e_{ji}e_{k}\otimes e_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{ijk}z_{ij}^{*}(\delta_{ik}e_{j}\otimes e_{k})=\sum_{ij}z_{ij}^{*}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})

and

\displaystyle(I\otimes Z^{(1)})M^{2,0}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{ij}I\otimes z_{ij}e_{ij}\right)M^{2,0}=\sum_{ijk}z_{ij}(e_{k}\otimes e_{ij}e_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{ijk}z_{ij}(e_{k}\otimes\delta_{jk}e_{i})=\sum_{ij}z_{ij}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i}).

Hence
\sum_{ij}z_{ij}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})=\sum_{ij}z_{ij}^{*}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})
, which implies
z_{ij}^{*}=z_{ij}
for all
i,j\in[n]
.

Finally, we are able to prove the converse of
Lemma

4.56
.

Corollary 4.75
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group and let
G
and
H
be graphs. If there exists a weak isomorphism
\Phi
between
Q(G)
and
Q(H)
, then there exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, such that
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M=M\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for all
M\in C^{Q}(k,\ell)
.

Proof 4.76
.

By
Theorem

4.73
, there exist a C
∗
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}
and matrices
Z^{(k)}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n^{k}\times n^{k}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

1.

Z^{(\ell)}T=\Phi(T)Z^{(k)}
for all
T\in C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
,

2.

Z^{(k)}\otimes Z^{(\ell)}=Z^{(k+\ell)}
,

3.

the entries
z_{ij}
of
Z^{(1)}
satisfy
z_{ij}^{*}=z_{ij}
.

Set
\mathcal{U}=Z^{(1)}
. Since
Z^{(1)}
is unitary, (c) implies that it is orthogonal.

Claim 5
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}=Z^{(k)}
.

Proof 4.77

(Proof)
.

First suppose
k=0
. We know that
Z^{(0)}
is a
1\times 1
matrix and thus of the form
(\lambda)
where
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}
. Moreover, by (b) we have
\lambda Z^{(1)}=Z^{(1)}\otimes Z^{(0)}=Z^{(1)}
. Since
Z^{(1)}
is unitary, multiplying both sides by
(Z^{(1)})^{*}
yields
\lambda I=I
, which implies
\lambda=\mathbf{1}
. Consequently,
Z^{0}=(\mathbf{1})=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}
.
By induction we then have

Z^{(k+1)}=Z^{(k)}\otimes Z^{(1)}=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes(k+1)}.

Finally suppose
M\in C^{Q}(\ell,k)\subseteq C^{Q}_{G}(\ell,k)
. Then by (a) we have

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}M=Z^{(\ell)}M=\Phi(M)Z^{(k)}=MZ^{(k)}=M\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}.

Since
A_{G}\in C^{Q}_{G}(1,1)
, we analogously get
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
as desired.

5
Cycles and More: Constructing Intertwiner Graphs

In the previous two sections we have established the following homomorphism indistinguishability characterisation.

See
1

Proof 5.78
.

We proved 1
\Rightarrow
2 in
Lemma

3.47
, 2
\Rightarrow
1 in
Lemma

3.54
, and 2
\Rightarrow
3 is in
Corollary

4.75
. The implication 3
\Rightarrow
4 is trivial, and 4
\Rightarrow
2 is proved in
Lemma

4.56
. Finally, 2
\Rightarrow
5 is proved in
Lemma

4.61
.

The natural question to ask is what the intertwiner graph classes of the orthogonal quantum groups actually look like. Mančinska and Roberson showed that for the quantum symmetric group one obtains the class of planar bilabelled graphs, whose
(0,0)
-fragment are simply the planar graphs. In this section, we answer this question for the remaining orthogonal easy quantum groups. While Mančinska and Roberson identified the entire class of intertwiner graphs, we will here only focus on the subclass
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
of unlabelled graphs in
\mathcal{F}
. This simplifies our presentation somewhat, and is sufficient to state the resulting homomorphism indistinguishability characterisations. We find that the resulting graph classes are either all graphs, all planar graphs, all cycles, or all paths and cycles. To structure this section, we follow the classification of
Theorem

2.21
.

In the following we understand all mentioned classes to be closed under disjoint unions. This is because for any two constructed graphs
\mathbf{K},\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
, their disjoint union is
\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
, and thus always constructible. In the context of homomorphism indistinguishability this makes no difference, since
\hom(F_{1}\cup F_{2},G)=\hom(F_{1},G)\cdot\hom(F_{2},G)
for all graphs
F_{1},F_{2},G
.
For brevity, we refrain from drawing all the involved intermediate and gadget graphs, and only highlight a few central ones in
Figures

7
and
10
. However, most of the constructions should be clear from a quick sketch on a piece of paper.

5.1
Classical, Liberated, and Half-Liberated Quantum Groups

Let us begin by investigating the classical, liberated, and the half-liberated quantum groups—the latter being the easy quantum groups
G_{n}(\mathcal{C})
with
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to56.91pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
