\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 39.37 62.99 M 39.37 0 L 0 62.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\not\in\mathcal{C}
.

The first observation is that the bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
, and
\mathbf{A}
already allow us to construct arbitrary cycles. Since every easy quantum group has
I
and
M^{2,0}
as intertwiners, their corresponding intertwiner graphs always include all cycles. In particular, the class of unlabelled intertwiner graphs of the quantum orthogonal group—which is generated precisely by
I
and
M^{2,0}
—is exactly the class of all cycles. Moreover, the swap graph
\mathbf{S}
does not allow us to construct any additional graphs, so the same holds for the classical orthogonal group, generated by
I,M^{2,0},S
.

Lemma 5.79
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the orthogonal group and
\mathcal{F}^{+}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the quantum orthogonal group. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{F}^{+}(0,0)=\mathcal{C}
, the class of all cycles.

Proof 5.80
.

We have
\mathcal{F}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
and
\mathcal{F}^{+}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
. We first show that we can construct arbitrary cycles. We have
C_{1}=\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
and for
n\geq 2
,

C_{n}=\mathbf{M}^{0,2}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{n-1})\mathbf{M}^{2,0}.

To see that
\mathcal{F}(0,0)\supseteq\mathcal{F}^{+}(0,0)
only contains cycles, we show that every vertex has degree
2
, or is part of either
C_{1}
or
C_{2}
. For finite graphs, this is equivalent to being a disjoint union of cycles.
Let
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}
and denote for
\mathbf{K}^{v}
the connected component containing
v\in V(\mathbf{K})
. By induction, we show the slightly stronger statement that all vertices
v\in V(\mathbf{K})
satisfy either
\deg(v)+\lvert l(v)\rvert=2
,
\mathbf{K}^{v}=C_{1}
, or
\mathbf{K}^{v}=C_{2}
.

Obviously this holds for the generators
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}
, so assume it holds for
\mathbf{K}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
. The inductive step is trivial for
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{2}
and
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}^{*}
, so assume
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\mathbf{K}_{2}
. The composition leaves unlabelled vertices unchanged, and by assumption identifies at most
3
vertices at a time. Let
v,v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbf{K}_{1}
and
w\in\mathbf{K}_{2}
. We make the following case distinction.

\lvert l_{out}(v)\rvert=\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=2
: In this case
v
and
w
must be isolated vertices, and thus their identification
vw
is an isolated vertex without labels. Consequently,
\mathbf{K}^{vw}=C_{1}
.

\lvert l_{out}(v)\rvert=\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=1
: In this case
v
and
w
have exactly one adjacent vertex in
\mathbf{K}_{1}
and
\mathbf{K}_{2}
respectively, so
vw
is a labelless vertex of degree
2
.

\lvert l_{out}(v_{1})\rvert=\lvert l_{out}(v_{2})\rvert=1
,
\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=2
: In this case
w
is an isolated vertex, and
v_{1},v_{2}
both have exactly one adjacent vertex in
\mathbf{K}_{1}
. Denote the adjacent vertices by
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
v_{2}^{\prime}
respectively. If
v_{1}^{\prime}\neq v_{2}^{\prime}
, then
v_{1}v_{2}w
is a labelless vertex of degree 2 as desired. If
v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq v^{\prime}
, then
\deg(v^{\prime})=2
. By assumption this implies that
v^{\prime}
is labelless and has no other neighbours. Consequently,
\mathbf{K}^{v^{\prime}}=\mathbf{K}^{v_{1}v_{2}w}=C_{2}
.

The remaining case is symmetric.

Obviously, this also holds for quantum groups
Q
with
O_{n}\subseteq Q\subseteq O^{+}_{n}
.

Corollary 5.81
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of
G_{n}(E_{o})
. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{C}
, the class of all cycles.

Proof 5.82
.

We have
E_{o}=\langle\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to56.91pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 59.06 62.99 M 29.53 0 L 29.53 62.99 M 59.06 0 L 0 62.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\rangle
. Let
\mathbf{\bar{S}}
denote the bilabelled graph corresponding to

. Then
\mathcal{F}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{\bar{S}}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
, and we have

\mathbf{\bar{S}}=(\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{I})\circ(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{S})\circ(\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{I}).

In particular, we get

\mathcal{C}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{\bar{S}},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}=\mathcal{C}
.

Along the same lines as
Lemma

5.79
, we can prove the following result for the bistochastic and quantum bistochastic groups.

Lemma 5.83
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the bistochastic group and
\mathcal{F}^{+}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the quantum bistochastic group. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{F}^{+}(0,0)
is the class of all paths and cycles.

Proof 5.84
.

We have
\mathcal{F}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{1,0},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
and
\mathcal{F}^{+}=\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{1,0},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
. We proceed similarly to the previous lemma. We already know how to construct arbitary cycles.
P_{1}
is again equal to
\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
, and for
n\geq 2
, we have

P_{n}=\mathbf{M}^{0,1}\circ\mathbf{A}^{n-1}\circ\mathbf{M}^{1,0}.

To see that
\mathcal{F}(0,0)\supseteq\mathcal{F}^{+}(0,0)
only contains paths and cycles, we show that every vertex has degree at most
2
. For finite graphs, this is equivalent to being a disjoint union of paths and cycles. We show the slightly stronger statement that for all
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{F}
all vertices
v\in V(K)
satisfy
\deg(v)+\lvert l(v)\rvert\leq 2
.

Obviously, this holds for
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{1,0},\mathbf{S}
, so assume it holds for
\mathbf{K}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
. The inductive step is again trivial for
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{2}
and
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}^{*}
, so assume
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\mathbf{K}_{2}
. The composition leaves unlabelled vertices unchanged and by assumption identifies at most
3
vertices at a time. Let
v,v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbf{K}_{1}
and
w\in\mathbf{K}_{2}
. We make the following case distinction.

\lvert l_{out}(v)\rvert=\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=2
: In this case
v
and
w
must be isolated vertices, and thus their identification
vw
is an isolated vertex without labels. In particular,
\deg(vw)+\lvert l(vw)\rvert=0
.

\lvert l_{out}(v)\rvert=\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=1
: In this case
v
and
w
have at most one adjacent vertex in
V(K_{1})
and
V(K_{2})
respectively. It follows that
vw
is an unlabelled vertex with degree
\deg(v)+\deg(w)\leq 2
.

\lvert l_{out}(v_{1})\rvert=\lvert l_{out}(v_{2})\rvert=1
,
\lvert l_{in}(w)\rvert=2
: In this case
w
is an isolated vertex, and
v_{1},v_{2}
both have at most one adjacent vertex in
\mathbf{K}_{1}
. Thus
v_{1}v_{2}w
is an unlabelled vertex with
\deg(v_{1}v_{2}w)\leq\deg(v_{1})+\deg(v_{2})\leq 2
.

The remaining case is symmetric.

Since paths are fairly easy to construct, we obtain the following result for supergroups of the (quantum) bistochastic group.

Lemma 5.85
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group and
\mathcal{F}
the class of its intertwiner graphs. If
Q
is either

1.

the bistochastic group
B_{n}
,

2.

the quantum bistochastic group
B_{n}^{+}
,

3.

the modified bistochastic group
B_{n}^{\prime}
,

4.

the quantum modified bistochastic group
B_{n}^{\#+}
,

5.

the quantum complexly modified bistochastic group
B_{n}^{\prime+}
, or

6.

G_{n}(E^{\prime}_{b})
,

then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all paths and cycles.

Proof 5.86
.

The cases 1 and 2 are proved in
Lemma

5.83
. For the remaining groups, their definining partition categories are either subsets of those defining the bistochastic group or those defining the quantum bistochastic group (depending on whether they contain crossing partitions). This means that their intertwiner graphs must be disjoint unions of paths or cycles. Moreover, the partition categories are obviously supersets of those defining the orthogonal group, so we can construct arbitrary cycles. Consequently, it suffices to show that we can also construct arbitrary paths.

For
B_{n}^{\prime}
,
B_{n}^{\#+}
, and
G_{n}(E^{\prime}_{b})
we have
\mathbf{M}^{1,0}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{1,0}\in\mathcal{F}
. This allows us to construct
P_{n}
as

P_{n}=(\mathbf{M}^{0,1}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,1})\circ(\mathbf{A}^{n}\otimes\mathbf{I})\circ\mathbf{M}^{2,0}.

For
B_{n}^{\prime+}
, we instead have access to
\mathbf{M}^{1,0}\otimes\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,1}
, which allows us to construct
P_{n}
as

\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\circ(\mathbf{M}^{1,0}\otimes\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,1})\circ(\mathbf{A}^{n}\otimes\mathbf{I})\circ\mathbf{M}^{2,0}.

=

Figure 7:
Constructing the edge gadget
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
.

The remaining easy quantum groups enjoy much larger classes of intertwiner graphs. This is in part due to a general construction that allows us to compose any unlabelled graph when we have access to
\mathbf{M}^{2,2}
and some “swapping” partition.

Lemma 5.87
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the class of all (unlabelled) graphs. Then
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{S}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
and
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{\bar{S}}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
.

Proof 5.88
.

We first show that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{\bar{S}}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
. Let
G\in\mathcal{G}
with
\lvert G\rvert\eqqcolon n
. We construct
G
as follows. Fix some order
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
on
V(G)
and start with
n
copies of
\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
that we identify with the vertices of
G
. We now construct two gadgets. The first,
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
, allows us to add an edge between vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
for all
i\in[n-1]
. First note that
\mathbf{M}^{4,2}=(\mathbf{M}^{2,2}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,2})\circ(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,0}\otimes\mathbf{I})
. We let

\hat{\mathbf{A}}=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,4})\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2})\circ(\mathbf{M}^{4,2}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}).

Composition with
\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(i-1)}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(n-i-1)}
then adds an edge between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
while preserving the out-labels. The second gadget allows us to swap any two vertices adjacent in the ordering. We let

\hat{\mathbf{S}}=(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{\bar{S}})\circ(\mathbf{\bar{S}}\otimes\mathbf{I}).

Composition with
\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(i-1)}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{S}}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(n-i-1)}
swaps
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
. More precisely, let
\mathbf{K}=(K,(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{n},v_{n}),())
. Then it is straightforward to verify that

\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{i}\circ\mathbf{K}=(K,(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{i+1},v_{i+1},v_{i},v_{i},\dots,v_{n},v_{n}),()).

Together, these two gadgets allow us to add edges between arbitrary vertices. After adding the edges, we can remove the
2n
out-labels by composition with
(\mathbf{M}^{0,2})^{\otimes n}
.
Finally, since

\mathbf{\bar{S}}=(\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{I})\circ(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{S})\circ(\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{I}),

we get that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{\bar{S}}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}\subseteq\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{S}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
.

In the context of the classification results of
Section

2.3
, we can thus state the following corollary.

Corollary 5.89
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group and
\mathcal{F}
the class of its intertwiner graphs. If
Q
is either

1.

the symmetric group
S_{n}
,

2.

the hyperoctahedral group
H_{n}
,

3.

the modified symmetric group
S^{\prime}_{n}
,

4.

G_{n}(E_{h})
, or

5.

G_{n}(E_{h}^{s})
,

then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{G}
, the class of all graphs.

5.2
The Case of Planar Graphs

In the case of the quantum modified symmetric- and the quantum hyperoctahedral group, the
(0,0)
-intertwiner graphs turn out to be all planar graphs. In fact, an approach analogous to Mančinska and Roberson’s proof for the quantum symmetric group goes through if we replace single labels by double labels. Their proof can be found in Sections 5 and 6 of
[
14
]
, we keep our proof slightly more concise.

We first need a notion of planarity for bilabelled graphs. The idea is to not only consider the underlying graph, but also require the wires representing the labels not to cross in any embedding. The way to formalise this is to turn wires into full-edges by adding the missing incident vertices, enforce their ordering by connecting them in a cycle, and then reason about the planarity of the resulting (classical) graph.

Definition 5.90
.

For a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
we define
K^{\circ}\coloneqq K^{\circ}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
as the graph obtained from
K
by adding the cycle
C=\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k},\beta_{\ell},\dots,\beta_{1}
and adding the edges
a_{i}\alpha_{i}
and
b_{j}\beta_{j}
for all
i\in[k]
and
j\in[\ell]
. We refer to
C
as the
enveloping cycle
2
2

2

In the case
k+\ell=2
, Mančinska and Roberson take the enveloping cycle to consist of two vertices connected by two edges, hence
K^{\circ}
is a multigraph. This is done to avoid special cases in their proofs and does not change the definition of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
.
of
K^{\circ}
.
Moreover, we define
K^{\odot}\coloneqq K^{\odot}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
by adding an additional vertex adjacent to all vertices on the enveloping cycle.

Although
K^{\circ}
and
K^{\odot}
depend on the input and output vectors of
\mathbf{K}
, they will usually be clear from the context, so we will omit them in our notation. Using these graphs, we can define planar bilabelled graphs.

Definition 5.91
.

For any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the planar
(k,\ell)
-bilabelled graphs are given by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(k,\ell)=\{\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)\mid K^{\odot}\text{ is planar}\},

and we let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(k,\ell)
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(0,0)
are just the planar graphs.

The additional vertex in
K^{\odot}
ensures that there exists an embedding where the enveloping cycle is the boundary of the outer face. In such an embedding, the new edges really correspond to the original wires. Cycles with this property are called
facial
.
Hence, an alternative definition of planar bilabelled graphs is the following.

Lemma 5.92
.

For any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the planar
(k,\ell)
-bilabelled graphs are given by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(k,\ell)=\{\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)\mid K^{\circ}\text{ is planar and its enveloping cycle is facial}\}.

The proof is now by induction on the size of the graph. Concretely, one can show that any planar bilabelled graph contains a vertex, which, when removed, can be added back through a collection of planar bilabelled gadget graphs and the usual operations of composition, tensor products, and taking adjoints.

Definition 5.93
.

Let
S^{d}
be the star graph on
d+1
vertices with vertex set
\{v,v_{1},\dots,v_{d}\}
and edges
\{vv_{i}\mid i\in[d]\}
. For all
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\coloneqq(S^{2d},(v^{2m}),(v_{1},\dots,v_{2d}))
,
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}\coloneqq(S^{2d},(v^{2m}),(v,v,v_{1},\dots,v_{2d}))
, and
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R}\coloneqq(S^{2d},(v^{2m}),(v_{1},\dots,v_{2d},v,v))
.

Our gadget graphs differ from those introduced in
[
14
]
in the duplication of all labels. We let
{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell,k)
denote the class of planar bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{K}
of the form
(K,(a_{1},a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},a_{\ell}),

(b_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{k},b_{k}))
.

For the inductive step, we need certain basic operations on bilabelled graphs that preserve planarity. The proofs are straightforward, so we omit them here.

Lemma 5.94
.

Let
G
be a planar graph,
u,v\in V(G)
, and
uv\in E(G)
. If
G^{\prime}
is a graph obtained from
G
by either removing
u
, removing
uv
, subdividing
uv
, or contracting
uv
, then
G^{\prime}
is planar.

Lemma 5.95
.

Let
G
be a planar graph,
u,v,w\in V(G)
, and
uv,vw\in E(G)
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by subdividing the edge
vw
with vertex
w_{1}
, and adding the edge
uw_{1}
. Then
G^{\prime}
is planar.

Lemma 5.96

(Lemma 4.11 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
G
be a planar graph and
v\in V(G)
, such that there exists a planar embedding in which the edges incident to
v
occur in cyclic order
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
. Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by subdividing each
e_{i}
with vertices
v_{i}
and adding the edges
v_{k}v_{1}
and
v_{i}v_{i+1}
for
i<k
. Then
G^{\prime}
is planar.

Lemma 5.97

(Lemma 4.12 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
G
and
H
be planar graphs with facial cycles
C=u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
and
D=v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}
respectively. Let
X
be the graph obtained from
G\cup H
by adding the edges
u_{1}v_{1},\dots u_{m}v_{m}
for some
m\leq\min(k,\ell)
. Then
X
is planar and
u_{m},u_{m+1},\dots,u_{\ell},u_{1},v_{1},v_{k},v_{k-1},\dots,v_{m}
is a facial cycle.

With slightly more work, one can show that composition, tensor products, and adjoint can, in fact, be reduced to these operations.

Lemma 5.98

(cf. Section 5.1 in
[
14
]
)
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
is closed under composition, tensor products, and taking adjoints.

We now note a necessary condition regarding the labels of a planar bilabelled graph.
Recall the bijection
\Gamma
between partitions and edgeless bilabelled graphs introduced in
Section

2.4
. We can extend the domain of
\Gamma
to arbitrary bilabelled graphs by simply ignoring their edges.
A bit of thought then shows that any partition associated to a planar bilabelled graph in this way must be non-crossing.

Lemma 5.99

(Lemma 6.3 in
[
14
]
)
.

For any bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
, the partition
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{K}}
is non-crossing.

Central to our construction of planar bilabelled graphs will be the following technical statements about non-crossing partitions. To state them concisely we consider indices modulo
n
.

Lemma 5.100

(Lemma 6.4 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
V
be a set and let
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\in V^{n}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{c}}
is non-crossing. If there exists a
v\in V
and
i,r\in[n]
such that
c_{i}=v=c_{i+r}
and
c_{i+s}\neq v
for all
s\in[r-1]
, then there exists a
u\in V
that occurs consecutively in
\mathbf{c}
and only occurs among
c_{i+1},\dots,c_{i+r-1}
.

Corollary 5.101

(Corollary 6.5 in
[
14
]
)
.

Let
V
be a set and
\mathbf{c}\in V^{n}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{c}}
is non-crossing. Then there exists a
v\in V
that occurs consecutively in
\mathbf{c}
. In particular, if
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{c}}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{K}}
for some
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in\mathcal{P}(\ell,k)
, then there exists a vertex
v\in V(K)
that occurs consecutively in
(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},b_{k},\dots,b_{1})
.

In particular, these results imply that for any planar bilabelled graph, there must exist a vertex whose labels are not “interleaved” with the remaining labels. The central observation is that we can add such a vertex to a planar bilabelled graph using the gadget graphs introduced in Definition
5.93
. This allows us to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.102
.

Let
\mathbf{K}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
with
V(\mathbf{K})\geq 2
. Then there exists a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
with
\lvert V(\mathbf{K}^{\prime})\rvert=\lvert V(\mathbf{K})\rvert-1
and
m,d,r,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that one of the following holds:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2r}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime}

(5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2r}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})^{*}

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=({{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})\circ(\mathbf{M}^{2,2r}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{\prime})

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R})\circ(\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,2r})

(8)

Proof 5.103
.

Suppose first that
\mathbf{K}=(K,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}(\ell,k)
with
\ell+k\geq 1
. By
Corollary

5.101
, there exists a
v\in V(\mathbf{K})
that occurs consecutively in
(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},b_{k},\dots,b_{1})
. We will construct
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
by removing
v
. We make the following case distinction.

Case 1:
v
occurs only in
\mathbf{a}
. In this case,
v
might occur consecutively by appearing in
a_{1},\dots,a_{p}
and
a_{q},\dots,a_{\ell}
for some
p,q\in[\ell]
. But if this is the case, we can find, by
Corollary

5.101
, a
v^{\prime}\in V(\mathbf{K})
that occurs consecutively and only among
a_{p+1},\dots a_{q-1}
. We may thus assume that the occurrences of
v
in
\mathbf{a}
are precisely
a_{p},\dots,a_{q}
for some
p,q\in[\ell]
.
Now let
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
be the neighbours of
v
, and set
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(K^{\prime},\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b})
, where
K^{\prime}=K-v
and
\mathbf{a^{\prime}}=(a_{1},a_{1},\dots,a_{p-1},v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{d},v_{d},a_{q+1},\dots,a_{\ell})
. Then

\mathbf{K}=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2r}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime},

where
r=p-1
,
m=q-p+1
, and
t=\ell-q
.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
is planar. This is proven by constructing
K^{\prime\circ}
from
K^{\circ}
using the operations listed in
Lemmas

5.94
and
5.96
. For brevity, we show this procedure graphically in
Figure

8
. Compared to the proof in
[
14
]
, we need an additional step to duplicate the out-labels of
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
.

Case 2:
v
occurs only in
\mathbf{b}
. In this case
\mathbf{K}^{*}
falls into Case 1. Consequently, there exists a
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
such that

\mathbf{K}^{*}=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2r}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime}.

Taking the adjoint of both sides yields

\mathbf{K}={\mathbf{K}^{\prime}}^{*}\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2r}\otimes{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})^{*},

and it is not hard to see that
{\mathbf{K}^{\prime}}^{*}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Case 3:
a_{1}=b_{1}=v
. In this case
v
occurs in both
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}
, so we need to do slightly more work to ensure the correct labelling. First we consider the special subcase where
a_{\ell}=b_{k}=v
, but not all entries of
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}
are equal to
v
. In that case there exists by
Corollary

5.101
a
v^{\prime}\in V(\mathbf{K})
that occurs consecutively and only among
\mathbf{a}
or
\mathbf{b}
. Thus either Case 1 or Case 2 apply.

Otherwise, we can assume that the occurrences of
v
are exactly
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{r}
respectively. Again let
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
be the neighbours of
v
in
\mathbf{K}
and set
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(K^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
, where
K^{\prime}=K-v
,
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{d},v_{d},a_{m+1},a_{m+1},\dots,a_{\ell})
, and
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=(b_{r+1},b_{r+1},\dots,b_{k})
. Let
t=\ell-m
. Then we have

\mathbf{K}=({{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2t})\circ(\mathbf{M}^{2,2r}\otimes\mathbf{K^{\prime}}).

To see that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
is planar, we reduce to Case 1. Consider
\mathbf{\hat{K}}=\mathbf{K}\circ(\mathbf{M}^{2r,0}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(k-r)})
. This removes the occurrences of
v
from
\mathbf{b}
, so it falls into Case 1. Moreover, the
\mathbf{\hat{K}}^{\prime}
constructed in Case 1 is exactly the
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
constructed here. In particular,
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Case 4:
a_{\ell}=b_{k}=v
. This case is symmetric to Case 3.

Case 5:
k+\ell=0
. In this case there is no enveloping cycle. We thus choose an arbitrary vertex
v\in V(\mathbf{K})
with neighbours
v_{1},\dots v_{d}
and let
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}=(K^{\prime},(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{d},v_{d}),())
with
K^{\prime}=K-v
. Then

\mathbf{K}={{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{0,2d}\circ\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
. To see that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
is planar, fix some planar embedding of
K
where
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
appear in cyclic order. Subdivide the edges
vv_{i}
with vertices
\alpha_{i}
and add edges to create a cycle
C=\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d}
. By
Lemma

5.96
, the resulting graph is planar and is in fact isomorphic to
K^{\prime\odot}
. It follows that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Proving that
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
.

The lemma now allows us to inductively construct our desired planar graphs.

Lemma 5.104
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the quantum hyperoctahedral group, and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of the quantum modified symmetric group. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(0,0)=\mathcal{P}
, the class of all planar graphs.

Proof 5.105
.

We show the stronger result that
{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
by induction. The one-vertex graphs in
{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
are precisely the
\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}
for
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
. The induction base thus follows from the next claim.

Claim 6
.

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}\in\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
.

Proof 5.106

(Proof)
.

Suppose we already have
\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}
for some
r,s\geq 1
. Then we can construct
\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s+2}
and
\mathbf{M}^{2r+2,2s}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s+2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(2r-1)}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\otimes\mathbf{I})\circ(\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,2}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{2r+2,2s}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,2})\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(2s-1)}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,0}\otimes\mathbf{I}).

It thus follows by induction that we can construct
\mathbf{M}^{2r,2s}
for all
r,s\geq 1
. Finally, we have
\mathbf{M}^{2r,0}=\mathbf{M}^{2r,2}\circ\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
and
\mathbf{M}^{0,2s}=(\mathbf{M}^{2s,0})^{*}
, which completes the proof.

Now consider some
\mathbf{K}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
with
V(\mathbf{K})\geq 2
. By
Lemma

5.102
, there exists a bilabelled graph
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}\in{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}
with
\lvert V(\mathbf{K}^{\prime})\rvert=\lvert V(\mathbf{K})\rvert-1
such that one of
Equations

5
,
6
,
7
and
8
holds. By induction hypothesis we can construct
\mathbf{K}^{\prime}
, so it suffices to show that we can construct
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}
,
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}
, and
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R}
.

Claim 7
.

Let
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d},{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L},{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R}\in\langle{}\mathbf{I},\mathbf{M}^{2,0},\mathbf{M}^{2,2},\mathbf{A}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
.

Proof 5.107

(Proof)
.

We have seen that we can construct
\mathbf{M}^{2m,2d}
, which allows us to construct
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}
as

{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}=\mathbf{M}^{2m,2d}\circ\mathbf{A}^{\otimes 2d}.

Similarly, we can construct
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}
and
{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R}
as

\displaystyle{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{L}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{2m,2d+2}\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{\otimes 2d}),

\displaystyle{{}^{2}\mathbf{S}}^{m,d}_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{2m,2d+2}\circ(\mathbf{A}^{\otimes 2d}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}).

We have thus shown that
{{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. In particular, this means that
\mathcal{P}={{}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}}(0,0)\subseteq\mathcal{F}(0,0)\cap\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(0,0)\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, where the second inclusion follows from the fact that the generators of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
are planar, and that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
is closed under composition, tensor products, and taking adjoints.

5.3
Group-Theoretical- and
\Pi
-Quantum Groups

It remains to investigate the (infinitely many) quantum groups induced by the partition categories in part (iv) and (v) of
Theorem

2.21
. We prove that the
(0,0)
-fragment of their respective intertwiner graphs contain all graphs.

The case for group-theoretical quantum groups follows from a result by Gromada
[
10
]
.
Let
\mathbf{K}
be a bilabelled graph and
\pi
be a partition of its vertex set. Then we denote by
\mathbf{K}/\pi
the graph obtained by identifying the blocks of
\pi
. Let
\mathbf{G}
denote the bilabelled graph associated to the partition

. Then the following result holds.

Lemma 5.108

(Lemma 2.31 in
[
10
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a superset of
\langle{}\mathbf{G}\rangle{}_{\circ,\otimes,*}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is closed under computing quotients
\mathbf{K}\mapsto\mathbf{K}/\pi
, where all unlabelled vertices of
\mathbf{K}
lie in singletons of
\pi
, i.e. are not identified in
\mathbf{K}/\pi
.

In particular, we can identify arbitrary labelled vertices. The general idea now is to start with
m
copies of
\mathbf{A}
and identify some of their endpoints. This way we can construct all graphs with
m
edges. However, we need to be slightly careful with this construction to be able to remove any remaining labels afterwards. We achieve this by using double labels.

Lemma 5.109
.

Let
Q
be a group-theoretical quantum group and
\mathcal{F}
the set of its intertwiner graphs. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all graphs.

Proof 5.110
.

We first construct a modified version of
\mathbf{A}
that has two in- and two out-labels. Note that
\mathbf{M}^{1,3}=(\mathbf{I}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2})\circ\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{M}^{2,4}=(\mathbf{M}^{1,3}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{1,3})\circ(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{2,0}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2})
. We then let

\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\coloneqq\mathbf{M}^{2,4}\circ\mathbf{A}^{\otimes 4}\circ\mathbf{M}^{4,2}.

We now show that we can construct any connected graph, the statement then follows from the fact that
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is closed under under tensor products.

First note that
K_{1}=\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\mathbf{M}^{2,0}\in\mathcal{F}(0,0)
.
Suppose then that
K
is a connected graph with
V(K)=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
,
n\geq 2
, and
\lvert E(K)\rvert=m
. We let
\mathbf{K}_{0}=\mathbf{\tilde{A}}^{\otimes m}
. Then
\mathbf{K}_{0}
has
2m
in-labels and out-labels respectively, and every vertex is labelled. We identify the
m
copies of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
with the edges
v_{i}v_{j}\in E(K)
and fix a function
\lambda\colon V(\mathbf{K}_{0})\to V(K)
mapping the vertices of
v_{i}v_{j}
to
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}
. We then define the partition
\pi=\{\{\lambda(w)=v\mid w\in V(\mathbf{K}_{0})\}\mid v\in V(K)\}
. Then
\mathbf{K}_{0}/\pi=(K,\mathbf{l},\mathbf{l})\eqqcolon\mathbf{K}
, where
\mathbf{l}
is of the form
(w^{v_{1}},w^{v_{1}},w^{v_{1}},w^{v_{1}},\dots,w^{v_{n}},w^{v_{n}})
, with an even number of copies of
w^{v_{i}}
for all
i\in[n]
and
\lvert\mathbf{l}\rvert=2m
. Hence,

K=(\mathbf{M}^{0,2})^{\otimes m}\circ\mathbf{K}\circ(\mathbf{M}^{2,0})^{\otimes m}.

yields the desired graph.

Finally, let us turn to the quantum groups
G_{n}(\Pi_{\infty})
and
G_{n}(\Pi_{k})
. First, we note that
\Pi_{1}=\langle\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, which corresponds to the quantum hyperoctahedral group and thus to the class of planar graphs.
For
G_{n}(\Pi_{2})
, we follow a similar approach as in
Lemma

5.87
. We will again start with
n
isolated vertices that each have two out-labels and then construct gadgets that allow us to add edges between arbitrary vertices. In fact, only the swap gadget will differ slightly.

Lemma 5.111
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of
G_{n}(\Pi_{2})
. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all graphs.

Proof 5.112
.

We first show that we have access to
\mathbf{M}^{2,2}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2}
denote the bilabelled graph associated to
\pi_{2}
. Then we can construct
\mathbf{M}^{4,0}
as

\mathbf{M}^{4,0}=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 4}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2})\circ\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2}.

\mathbf{M}^{2,2}
is now obtained by “rotating”
\mathbf{M}^{4,0}
: Observe that for any bilabelled graph
(F,(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
,
(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell}))\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k,\ell)
we can obtain the graph
(F,(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1}),

(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell},a_{k}))\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}(k-1,\ell+1)
by parallel composition with
\mathbf{I}
and then composing with
\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2}
.
Concretely, we have

\mathbf{M}^{2,2}=(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2})\circ\left(\left((\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 3}\otimes\mathbf{M}^{0,2})\circ(\mathbf{M}^{4,0}\otimes\mathbf{I})\right)\otimes\mathbf{I}\right).

We show this procedure in
Figure

9
, where we omit some of the identity graphs to improve legibility.
Recall then the construction of
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
from
Lemma

5.87
using
\mathbf{M}^{2,2}
and
\mathbf{A}
. Then
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
is an edge whose vertices both have two in-labels and out-labels respectively.
It remains to show that we can construct a graph that acts as a swap map. It turns out that we can construct a
fat swap

\mathbf{\tilde{S}}
that acts similarly to the
\mathbf{\hat{S}}
constructed in
Lemma

5.87
. We again achieve this by rotating
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2}
, see
Figure

10
.

Now to construct an arbitrary graph
K\in\mathcal{G}
with
\lvert G\rvert\eqqcolon n
, we again start with
n
copies of
\mathbf{M}^{2,0}
that we identify with the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V(G)
. Then composition with
\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(i-1)}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{A}}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(n-i-1)}
adds an edge between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
while preserving the out-labels.
Composition with
\mathbf{\tilde{S}}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(i-1)}\otimes\mathbf{\tilde{S}}\otimes\mathbf{I}^{\otimes 2(n-i-1)}
swaps
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
in the sense that for
\mathbf{K}=(K,(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{n},v_{n}),())
, we have

\mathbf{\tilde{S}}_{i}\circ\mathbf{K}=(K,(v_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{i+1},v_{i+1},v_{i},v_{i},\dots,v_{n},v_{n}),()).

Together, the gadgets
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
and
\mathbf{\tilde{S}}
thus allow us to add edges between arbitrary vertices. Finally, composition with
(\mathbf{M}^{0,2})^{\otimes n}
removes any remaining labels.

Constructing
\mathbf{M}^{2,2}
from
\mathbf{M}^{0,4}
.

=

Constructing the Fat Swap
\mathbf{\tilde{S}}
by rotating
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{2}
.

[

Corollary 5.114
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of intertwiner graphs of
G_{n}(\Pi_{k})
for
k\geq 2
and
\mathcal{F}^{\infty}
the class of intertwiner graphs of
G_{n}(\Pi_{\infty})
. Then
\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathcal{F}^{\infty}(0,0)=\mathcal{G}
, the class of all graphs.

Raum and Weber’s result moreover yields an alternative, less constructive, proof of
Lemma

5.109
.
We summarize the findings of this section in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.115
.

Let
Q
be an easy quantum group and
\mathcal{F}
the class of its intertwiner graphs. Then

1.

\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all graphs if
Q
is either

•

the symmetric group
S_{n}
,

•

the hyperoctahedral group
H_{n}
,

•

the modified symmetric group
S^{\prime}_{n}
,

•

G_{n}(E_{h})
,

•

G_{n}(E^{s}_{h})
,

•

a group-theoretical quantum group,

•

G_{n}(\Pi_{k})
for some
k\geq 2
, or

•

G_{n}(\Pi_{\infty})
.

2.

\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all planar graphs if
Q
is either

•

the quantum symmetric group
S^{+}_{n}
,

•

the quantum modified symmetric group
S_{n}^{\prime+}
, or

•

the quantum hyperoctahedral group
H_{n}^{+}
.

3.

\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all paths and cycles if
Q
is either

•

the bistochastic group
B_{n}
,

•

the quantum bistochastic group
B_{n}^{+}
,

•

the modified bistochastic group
B^{\prime}_{n}
,

•

the quantum modified bistochastic group
B_{n}^{\#+}
, or

•

the quantum complexly modified bistochastic group
B_{n}^{\prime+}
,

•

G_{n}(E^{\prime}_{b})
.

4.

\mathcal{F}(0,0)
is the class of all cycles if
Q
is either

•

the orthogonal group
O_{n}
,

•

the quantum orthogonal group
O_{n}^{+}
, or

•

G_{n}(E_{o})
.

Moreover, every
Q
falls into one of these cases.

6
A Characterisation of Orthogonal Easy Quantum Groups and Their Homomorphism Indistinguishability Relations

Up to this point, we have proved homomorphism indistinguishability results for all orthogonal easy quantum groups. We have also identified the corresponding classes of intertwiner graphs. Still, our theorem is stated in a rather abstract way in terms of intertwiners. In this final section, we categorise quantum matrices according to their intertwiners. This allows us to state several concrete corollaries of our theorem.

6.1
Properties of Quantum Matrices and Intertwiners

Our first observation is that the partitions used in the classification of easy quantum groups in fact correspond to very concrete properties of the fundamental representations.

Lemma 6.116
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a C
∗
-algebra and
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
. Then
\mathcal{U}
has intertwiner

1.

iff
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{1}I
,

2.

iff the entries of
\mathcal{U}
commute,

3.

\uparrow
iff
\mathcal{U}
has row sum
\mathbf{1}
,

4.

\downarrow
iff
\mathcal{U}
has column sum
\mathbf{1}
,

5.

\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
iff the product of any two row sums of
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathbf{1}
,

6.

\downarrow\otimes\downarrow
iff the product of any two column sums of
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathbf{1}
,

7.

iff the entries
u_{ij}
of
\mathcal{U}
satisfy
u_{ik}u_{jk}=\delta_{ij}u_{ik}
for all
i,j,k\in[n]
,

8.

iff the entries
u_{ij}
of
\mathcal{U}
satisfy
u_{ki}u_{kj}=0
and
u_{ik}u_{jk}=0
for all
i,j,k\in[n]
with
i\neq j
.

Proof 6.117
.

We first show 1. Expanding
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}M^{2,0}=M^{2,0}(\mathbf{1})
yields

\sum_{i}\mathcal{U}e_{i}\otimes\mathcal{U}e_{i}=\sum_{i}\mathbf{1}(e_{i}\otimes e_{i}).

(9)

We thus have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{T}})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}u_{ik}u_{jk}=\sum_{k}(\mathcal{U}e_{k})_{i}\cdot(\mathcal{U}e_{k})_{j}=\Big(\sum_{k}\mathcal{U}e_{k}\otimes\mathcal{U}e_{k}\Big)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=(M^{2,0})_{i,j}\mathbf{1}=\delta_{ij}\mathbf{1}.

The remaining parts can all be proven along the same lines, by expanding the intertwiner equation and then comparing the entries of the resulting matrices. We show this again for case 2. Let
u_{*i}
and
u_{i*}
denote the
i
-th column and row vector of
\mathcal{U}
respectively.
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}S=S\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}
is equivalent to

\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}\Big(\sum_{i,j}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\Big)=\Big(\sum_{i,j}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\Big)\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}.

Expanding this yields

\sum_{i,j}(u_{*j}\otimes u_{*i})\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}=\sum_{i,j}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(u_{i*}\otimes u_{j*}),

and entrywise comparison gives

\displaystyle u_{i_{1}j_{2}}\cdot u_{i_{2}j_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}u_{i_{1}j}\cdot u_{i_{2}i}\cdot\delta_{ij_{1}}\cdot\delta_{jj_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i,j}(u_{*j}\otimes u_{*i})\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\Big)_{i_{1}i_{2},j_{1}j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i,j}(e_{j}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(u_{i*}\otimes u_{j*})\Big)_{i_{1}i_{2},j_{1}j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\delta_{i_{1}j}\cdot\delta_{i_{2}i}\cdot u_{ij_{1}}\cdot u_{jj_{2}}

\displaystyle=u_{i_{2}j_{1}}\cdot u_{i_{1}j_{2}}

for all
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in[n]
, as desired.

Now recall our definition of quantum permutation matrices as matrices with entries in some C
∗
-algebra, satisfying
Equation

2
, the definining relations of the quantum symmetric group. The previous lemma implies that we can rephrase this definition in terms of intertwiners.

Corollary 6.118
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a C
∗
-algebra. A matrix
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
is a quantum permutation matrix if and only if it has intertwiners
\uparrow,\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 31.5 M 0 31.5 L 39.37 31.5 M 39.37 31.5 L 39.37 0 M 19.69 31.5 L 19.69 55.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
.

Proof 6.119
.

We first note that if
\mathcal{U}
has
\uparrow
as an intertwiner then it also has
\downarrow
as an intertwiner, so the row and column sums are
\mathbf{1}
. It remains to show that the condition
u_{ik}u_{jk}=\delta_{ij}u_{ik}
for all
i,j,k\in[n]
is equivalent in this case to the weaker condition
u_{ij}^{2}=u_{ij}^{*}=u_{ij}
that we required in
Equation

2
.

To see this, suppose that
u^{2}_{ij}=u^{*}_{ij}=u_{ij}
and
\sum_{\ell}u_{\ell k}=\mathbf{1}
. Then

u_{jk}=u_{jk}\mathbf{1}u_{jk}=u_{jk}\Big(\sum_{\ell}u_{\ell k}\Big)u_{jk}=u_{jk}+\sum_{\ell\neq j}u_{jk}u_{\ell k}u_{jk},

and thus
\sum_{\ell\neq j}u_{jk}u_{\ell k}u_{jk}=0
. Now note that all the terms
u_{jk}u_{\ell k}u_{jk}=(u_{\ell k}u_{jk})^{*}u_{\ell k}u_{jk}
are positive. Since the sum of positive elements is positive again, we also have that
-u_{jk}u_{ik}u_{jk}=\sum_{\ell\neq i,j}u_{jk}u_{\ell k}u_{jk}
is positive for all
k\in[n]
. Thus for
i\neq j
, both
u_{jk}u_{ik}u_{jk}
and
-u_{jk}u_{ik}u_{jk}
are positive, which is only possible if
u_{jk}u_{ik}u_{jk}=0
. But we then have

\lVert u_{ik}u_{jk}\rVert^{2}=\lVert(u_{ik}u_{jk})^{*}u_{ik}u_{jk}\rVert=\lVert u_{jk}u_{ik}u_{jk}\rVert=0.

It follows that
u_{ik}u_{jk}=0
for
i\neq j
. Moreover, per assumption we have
u_{ik}u_{ik}=u_{ik}
, so
u_{ik}u_{jk}=\delta_{ij}u_{ik}
as desired.

Through their intertwiners, we can also define quantum monomial, quantum signed permutation, quantum bistochastic, and quantum signed bistochastic matrices.

Definition 6.120
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a C
∗
-algebra. A non-zero matrix
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
whose entries
u_{ij}
satisfy
u^{*}_{ij}=u_{ij}
is called a

1.

quantum monomial orthogonal matrix
if it has intertwiners

,

,

2.

quantum signed permutation matrix
if it has intertwiners
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 36.22 M 0 36.22 L 39.37 36.22 M 39.37 36.22 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}},\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
,

,

3.

quantum bistochastic matrix
if it has intertwiners

,
\uparrow
,

4.

quantum signed bistochastic matrix
if it has intertwiners

,
\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
.

In particular, the fundamental representations of
H_{n}^{+}
,
S_{n}^{\prime+}
,
B_{n}^{+}
, and
B_{n}^{\#+}
are quantum monomial orthogonal-, quantum signed permutation-, quantum bistochastic-, and quantum signed bistochastic matrices respectively. In the same way as for quantum orthogonal and quantum permutation matrices, we recover the notions of monomial orthogonal, signed permutation, bistochastic, and signed bistochastic matrices when the C
∗
-algebra is
\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 6.121
.

Over
\mathbb{C}
, the following notions coincide

1.

monomial orthogonal matrices and quantum monomial orthogonal matrices,

2.

signed permutation matrices and quantum signed permutation matrices,

3.

bistochastic matrices and quantum bistochastic matrices,

4.

signed bistochastic matrices and quantum signed bistochastic matrices.

Proof 6.122
.

1: Recall that monomial matrices are matrices with the same non-zero pattern as a permutation matrix, whose non-zero values are arbitrary complex numbers. Using
Lemma

6.116
, it is straightforward to verify that every monomial orthogonal matrix has
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{},\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 62.99 L 0 44.09 M 39.37 62.99 L 39.37 44.09 M 0 44.09 L 39.37 44.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 18.9 L 19.69 44.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
as intertwiners and is thus a quantum monomial orthogonal matrix. Suppose then
\mathcal{U}=(u_{ij})
is quantum monomial orthogonal matrix over
\mathbb{C}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
has intertwiner

, it is orthogonal and in particular has at least one non-zero entry per row and column. Now suppose
u_{ik}\neq 0,u_{jk}\neq 0
for
i\neq j
. Since
u_{ik},u_{jk}\in\mathbb{C}
, this implies
u_{ik}u_{jk}\neq 0
contradicting—by
Lemma

6.116
—the fact that
\mathcal{U}
has

as an intertwiner. The case for
u_{ki}\neq 0,u_{kj}\neq 0
is analogous. Consequently,
\mathcal{U}
is orthogonal and has exactly one non-zero entry per row and column, making it a monomial orthogonal matrix.

2: It is again straightforward to verify that every signed permutation matrix is a quantum signed permutation matrix. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a quantum signed permutation matrix over
\mathbb{C}
. The same argument as for 1 shows that
\mathcal{U}
is a monomial orthogonal matrix. It thus suffices to show that the non-zero entries are all
1
or all
-1
. Since
\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
is an intertwiner,
Lemma

6.116
implies that the product of any two row sums must equal
1
. This implies that

1=\Big(\sum_{\ell}u_{i\ell}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\ell}u_{i\ell}\Big)=u_{ij}^{2}

for all
i
and some
j
. In particular, all non-zero entries must be
\pm 1
. Moreover, if there existed entries
u_{ij}=1
and
u_{k\ell}=-1
, then

\Big(\sum_{\ell}u_{i\ell}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\ell}u_{k\ell}\Big)=-1,

contrary to our observation. It follows that
\mathcal{U}
is a signed permutation matrix.

3: Follows immediately from
Lemma

6.116
.

4: It is easy to see that every signed bistochastic matrix is a quantum signed bistochastic matrix. Suppose then
\mathcal{U}
is quantum signed bistochastic matrix over
\mathbb{C}
. Then
\mathcal{U}
is orthogonal because it has

as an intertwiner. Moreover, the intertwiner
\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
guarantees that the product of any two row sums equals
1
. Along similar lines as in 2, this implies that every row sum must be
\pm 1
, and in fact all row sums must have the same value. Analogously, the intertwiner
\downarrow\otimes\downarrow
guarantees the same for the column sums. Finally, the rows and columns must sum to the same value, otherwise

\sum_{i,j}u_{ij}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}u_{ij}=\sum_{j}\Big(-\sum_{i}u_{ij}\Big)=-\sum_{i,j}u_{ij},

contradicting the fact that the sum of entries of
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is
\pm n
.
It follows that
\mathcal{U}
is a signed bistochastic matrix.

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
.

In particular, the fundamental representation of the quantum complexly modified bistochastic group
B^{{}^{\prime}+}_{n}
is a quantum complexly signed bistochastic matrix.
Since
\mathbb{C}
is commutative, we still recover signed bistochastic matrices when
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 6.124
.

Over
\mathbb{C}
, the notions of quantum complexly signed bistochastic matrices, quantum signed bistochastic matrices, and signed bistochastic matrices coincide.

Proof 6.125
.

Since
\mathbb{C}
is commutative, any quantum (complexly) signed bistochastic matrix over
\mathbb{C}
has

as an intertwiner. Since
\lower 2.84544pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{745860}{\hbox to42.68pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.11319pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, every quantum signed bistochastic matrix has

\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\rangle
, every quantum complexly signed bistochastic matrix has
\uparrow\otimes\uparrow
as an intertwiner and is thus a quantum signed bistochastic matrix. The statement now follows from
Lemma

6.121
.

Finally, for each of the quantum matrices above we obtain a version with commuting entries by additionally requiring that

be an intertwiner. In the context of compact matrix quantum groups, this intertwiner is precisely what makes a quantum group classical, and we can prove a similar result for quantum matrices.

Lemma 6.126
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a C
∗
-algebra, and
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
be a non-zero matrix with commuting entries. Then there exists a non-zero matrix
U\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
, such that
U^{\otimes k}T=T^{\prime}U^{\otimes\ell}
for all
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}\times n^{\ell}}
with
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
.

Proof 6.127
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
be the C
∗
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{Z}
generated by the entries
u_{ij}
of
\mathcal{U}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is commutative and thus by Gelfand-Naimark isomorphic to the function algebra
X
of some finite set
X
. More precisely,
X
is the set of non-zero
∗
-homomorphisms
\chi\colon\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}
and there exists an isomorphism
\phi\colon\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to C(X)
mapping
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
to

\tilde{z}\colon X\to\mathbb{C},\chi\mapsto\chi(z).

We now define a mapping
\rho\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
by
\rho(\chi)_{ij}=\phi(u_{ij})(\chi)=\chi(u_{ij})
. Since
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is non-trivial, there exists a
\chi\in X
. Set
U=\rho(\chi)
and let
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}\times n^{\ell}}
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for
k,\ell\geq 1
. Then since
\chi
is an algebra homomorphism, we have

\displaystyle\left(U^{\otimes k}T\right)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{1}\dots r_{k}}\left(\prod_{s}U_{i_{s}r_{s}}\right)T_{r_{1}\dots r_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{1}\dots r_{k}}\left(\prod_{s}\chi(u_{i_{s}r_{s}})\right)T_{r_{1}\dots r_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\sum_{r_{1}\dots r_{k}}\left(\prod_{s}u_{i_{s}r_{s}}\right)T_{r_{1}\dots r_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\left(\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T\right)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\left(T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}\right)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\sum_{r_{1}\dots r_{\ell}}T^{\prime}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k},r_{1}\dots r_{\ell}}\prod_{s}u_{r_{s}j_{s}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{1}\dots r_{\ell}}T^{\prime}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k},r_{1}\dots r_{\ell}}\prod_{s}U_{r_{s}j_{s}}

\displaystyle=\left(T^{\prime}U^{\otimes\ell}\right)_{i_{1}\dots r_{k},j_{1}\dots j_{\ell}}.

The case for
k+\ell\leq 1
follows from the observation that
\chi(\mathbf{1})=1
. Indeed, let
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
,
z\neq\mathbf{1}
,
z\neq 0
. Since
\chi
is a non-zero
∗
-homomorphism, we have
\chi(z)=\chi(z\cdot\mathbf{1})=\chi(z)\cdot\chi(\mathbf{1})
where
\chi(z)\neq 0
. Since
\chi(z),\chi(\mathbf{1})\in\mathbb{C}
,
\chi(\mathbf{1})
must be
1
. An analogous argument as above then shows that for
T\in\mathbb{C}^{1\times n^{\ell}}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}T=(\mathbf{1})T=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
implies
U^{\otimes 0}T=(1)T=T^{\prime}U^{\otimes\ell}
; for
T\in\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}\times 1}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}T=T^{\prime}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}=T^{\prime}(\mathbf{1})
implies
U^{\otimes k}T=T^{\prime}(1)=T^{\prime}U^{\otimes 0}
; and for
T\in\mathbb{C}^{1\times 1}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}T=(\mathbf{1})T=T^{\prime}(\mathbf{1})=\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 0}
implies
U^{\otimes 0}T=(1)T=T^{\prime}(1)=T^{\prime}U^{\otimes 0}
. This concludes the proof.

This means that we can essentially identify quantum matrices with commuting entries with classical matrices over
\mathbb{C}
that have the same intertwiners. In particular,
Lemmas

6.116
and
6.121
apply: For instance, if there exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
with commuting entries and
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, then there exists a corresponding orthogonal matrix
U
with
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

6.2
Homomorphism Indistinguishability

We have seen that in the cases of classical- and liberated quantum groups, the matrices in
1
either correspond to classical matrices or can be given meaningful names. Together with our results of
Section

5
, where we investigated the intertwiner graphs of the different orthogonal easy quantum groups, we can state various corollaries of
1
in a more readable form.

The first result is a new characterisation of homomorphism indistinguishability over the class of all graphs, which according to Lovász
[
11
]
is equivalent to graph isomorphism.

Corollary 6.128
.

For graphs
G
and
H
, the following are equivalent:

1.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of all graphs.

2.

There exists a permutation matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

3.

There exists a signed permutation matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

4.

There exists an orthogonal monomial matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

Moreover, we recover Mančinska and Roberson’s characterisation of quantum isomorphism, avoiding the detour through quantum orbits inherent to the original proof. We also obtain two additional, equivalent characterisations that mirror the classical case.

Corollary 6.129
.

For graphs
G
and
H
, the following are equivalent:

1.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of all planar graphs.

2.

There exists a quantum permutation matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

3.

There exists a quantum signed permutation matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

4.

There exists a quantum orthogonal monomial matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

Homomorphism indistinguishability over the class of all cycles is well-known to be equivalent to cospectrality, and hence the existence of an orthogonal matrix
U
with
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
. We recover this result and extend it by a quantum version.

Corollary 6.130
.

For graphs
G
and
H
, the following are equivalent:

1.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of all cycles.

2.

There exists an orthogonal matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

3.

There exists a quantum orthogonal matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

Finally, we recover and extend the characterisation of homomorphism indistinguishability over the class of all paths and cycles—noted by Seppelt
[
21
, Corollary 3.3.3]
and
[
26
, Theorem 3]
to correspond to the existence of a bistochastic matrix
U
with
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
—by the following criteria.

Corollary 6.131
.

For graphs
G
and
H
, the following are equivalent:

1.

G
and
H
are homomorphism indistinguishable over the class of all paths and cycles.

2.

There exists a bistochastic matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

3.

There exists a signed bistochastic matrix
U
such that
UA_{G}=A_{H}U
.

4.

There exists a quantum bistochastic matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

5.

There exists a quantum signed bistochastic matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

6.

There exists a quantum complexly signed bistochastic matrix
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
.

We finally note that the assertions of
Corollaries

6.128
,
6.130
,
6.131
and
6.129
are mutually not equivalent.
That is, homomorphism indistinguishability over cycles, cycles and paths, planar graphs, and all graphs
forms a descending chain of four pairwise distinct graph isomorphism relaxations.
This is implied by the stronger assertion that all (the disjoint union closures of) these graph classes are homomorphism distinguishing closed, cf.
[
18
, Theorems 4.7, 8.3]
and
[
21
, Theorem 7.1.4]
.
A graph class
\mathcal{F}
is
homomorphism distinguishing closed

[
18
]

if, for all
F\not\in\mathcal{F}
, there exist graphs
G
and
H
such that
G\cong_{\mathcal{F}}H
and
\hom(F,G)\neq\hom(F,H)
.

More generally, the characterisations in
Corollaries

6.128
,
6.130
,
6.131
and
6.129
make the graph isomorphism relaxations considered here subject to the emerging theory of homomorphism indistinguishability.
In particular, they imply that the graph isomorphism relaxations are preserved under certain operations
[
22
]

and that the notions in
Corollaries

6.130
and
6.131
can be decided in polynomial time
[
20
]
,
while those in
Corollary

6.129
are undecidable
[
1
,
12
]

and those in
Corollary

6.128
are in quasi-polynomial time
[
2
]
.

7
Conclusion

We studied the connection between orthogonal easy quantum groups and homomorphism indistinguishability.
We proved
1
, which characterises the feasability of the matrix equation
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, where
A_{G},A_{H}
are adjacency matrices of graphs
G
and
H
, and
\mathcal{U}
is a matrix indeterminate with entries in some C
∗
-algebra satisfying the relations
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M_{i}=M_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
for intertwiners
M_{i}
of some easy quantum group, as being equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over the unlabelled intertwiner graphs generated by the corresponding bilabelled graphs
\mathbf{M}_{i}
and the bilabelled edge
\mathbf{A}
. In particular, the theorem generalises Mančinska and Roberson’s characterisation of quantum isomorphism, in which case
\mathcal{U}
is a quantum permutation matrix, and the generated unlabelled intertwiner graphs are precisely the planar graphs.

The result is particularly nice because it joins a now quite extensive list of homomorphism indistinguishability relations characterising the feasability of matrix equations of the form
XA_{G}=A_{H}X
, cf.
[
21
, Table 1.2]
.
Salient examples of such relations include the existence of a pseudo-stochastic
X
, being equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over all paths, and the existence of doubly stochastic matrix
X
, being equivalent to homomorphism indistinguishability over all trees
[
5
]
.
In fact, we have shown that the conditions
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k}M_{i}=M_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
very naturally define well-known classes of matrices and corresponding quantum relaxations.
This allowed us to state some consequences of
1
in a form more closely resembling these existing homomorphism indistinguishability results.

7.1
The Limits of Quantum Groups for Homomorphism Indistinguishability

Interestingly, we reprove certain characterisations, namely those of the existence of orthogonal, permutation, and bistochastic solutions to the matrix equation
XA_{G}=A_{H}X
. Moreover, some homomorphism indistinguishability relations were already known for all the intertwiner graph classes of orthogonal quantum groups.

This suggests the question to what extent our results are exhaustive. The short answer is that we derived all homomorphism indistinguishability relations induced by orthogonal easy quantum groups. However, as Mančinska and Roberson show in
[
14
]
, orthogonal easy quantum groups are special instances of the more general graph-theoretic quantum groups. These are quantum groups whose intertwiners are given by homomorphism tensors of arbitrary bilabelled graphs. It turns out that our proof generalises to these more general quantum groups without much difficulty. The resulting homomorphism indistinguishability relations, however, characterise the existence of a solution
\mathcal{U}
not only to
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, but simultaneously for
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes k_{i}}\mathbf{K}^{(i)}_{G}=\mathbf{K}^{(i)}_{H}\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell_{i}}
—the
\mathbf{K}^{(i)}
being the
(k_{i},\ell_{i})
-bilabelled graphs whose homomorphism tensors span the intertwiners of the graph-theoretic quantum group in question. This seems like a rather arbitrary relation from a general point of view.

A more interesting class of quantum groups have been introduced by Tarrago and Weber in
[
25
]
and
[
24
]
. They introduce the
unitary easy quantum groups
which generalise the orthogonal easy quantum groups.
The unitary easy quantum groups are the quantum groups whose intertwiners can be characterised in terms of
coloured
partitions, where the elements of the underlying set are either black or white.
These coloured partitions correspond to linear maps in exactly the same way as uncoloured partitions, and hence can also be seen as homomorphism tensors of edgeless bilabelled graphs. Moreover, the operations of composition, tensor product, and taking adjoints are generally defined as for their uncoloured counterparts. Merely the composition requires that the bottom row of the first and the top row of the second partition additionally match in colours. In particular, the operations on bilabelled graphs and on coloured partitions coincide whenever they are defined on partitions. We can get rid of this caveat by considering bilabelled graphs with coloured labels, allowing composition only when label colours match. This, in turn, potentially restricts the set of intertwiner graphs we are able to construct. Under these definitions, our proofs go through with some obvious minor changes. The remaining task would be to investigate which concrete graph classes result from these definitions.

As the name suggests, the fundamental representations of unitary easy quantum groups do not have to be orthogonal, which makes them a promising candidate to obtain homomorphism indistinguishability relations coarser than the ones derived in this paper.

7.2
Multigraphs

An indispensible tool in our construction of the intertwiner graph classes of orthogonal easy quantum groups is the suppression of parallel edges. This, however, has the drawback that the resulting graph classes collapse into essentially four cases. Therefore, it might be enlightening to consider the classes of multigraphs generated when keeping duplicate edges. For the results to fit into our framework of homomorphism indistinguishability, however, we have to count homomorphisms from multigraphs. One option to do this is to define

\hom(F,G)=\sum_{f\colon V(F)\to V(G)}\prod_{uv\in E(F)}m_{E(G)}(f(u)f(v)),

where
m_{E(G)}\colon E(G)\to\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity function of the multiset of edges
E(G)
. For bilabelled graphs
(F,\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
and
(G,\mathbf{a^{\prime}},\mathbf{b^{\prime}})
, we instead sum only over functions
f\colon V(F)\to V(G)
with
f(\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
and
f(\mathbf{b})=\mathbf{b^{\prime}}
.
Under this definition, the
uv
-entry of
\mathbf{A}_{G}
is exactly equal to the number of edges between
u
and
v
, which fits the usual definition of the adjacency matrix of a multigraph. Moreover, as Mančinska and Roberson note in the conclusion of
[
14
]
, allowing multiedges does not break any of the correspondence results between homomorphism tensors, linear maps, and partitions.

An interesting caveat with respect to multigraphs is also noted by Mančinska and Roberson. Namely, there is no established definition of the quantum automorphism group of a multigraph
G
. Proceeding as for simple graphs, by simply requiring that
\mathcal{U}A_{G}=A_{H}\mathcal{U}
, would mean that all of our proofs go through nearly unchanged. However, this might not be the natural choice. In the classical case, we usually want to capture the permutation of parallel edges in our automorphisms, while the above definition only captures the action on vertices.

On the other hand, it is not clear that trying to mirror the classical automorphism group of multigraphs is the correct approach in the first place. To illustrate this point, recall that a central step in our proof is to add the adjacency matrix
A_{G}
to the intertwiners of an easy quantum group. For classical groups, this is equivalent to requiring that
UA_{G}=A_{G}U
for all its elements
U
, which for the symmetric group happens to yield the automorphism group
\Aut(G)
of
G
. However, it is
generally not clear what it means to require that the elements
U
of a matrix group satisfy
UA_{G}=A_{G}U
. The symmetric group is the exception, since it acts on graphs by conjugating the adjacency matrix.
UA_{G}=A_{G}U
, being equivalent to
UA_{G}U^{-1}=A_{G}
, thus simply says that the adjacency matrix has to be invariant under the group action, which leaves precisely the permutations that are automorphisms. For other matrix groups, this conjugation does not define a group action, since
UA_{G}U^{-1}
is generally not an adjacency matrix anymore. Depending on whether there is a natural interpretation of the subgroup of a matrix group defined by the relation
UA_{G}=A_{G}U
, simply adding
A_{G}
as an intertwiner might ultimately prove to be a more natural choice. In fact, whether such an interpretation exists is an interesting question in its own right.
